ABFM Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Symptomatic care with fluids, rest, and analgesics as needed): uncomplicated viral upper respiratory infection at day two without red flags requires only supportive care. Antibiotics offer no benefit against a viral process and promote resistance. Sinus imaging is unnecessary this early and would not change management. Steroids are not indicated for a routine viral URI and carry unnecessary risk.
  2. The correct answer is D (Clear rhinorrhea that briefly turns yellow-tinged on day three): transient discolored discharge early in a viral URI is a normal part of the inflammatory course, not a sign of bacterial superinfection. A biphasic pattern with worsening after initial improvement, sometimes called double sickening, is the classic red flag for secondary bacterial sinusitis. Persistent mild cough into week two is common after viral URIs. Slow congestion improvement alone is not concerning.
  3. The correct answer is A (Community-acquired pneumonia with a focal lobar consolidation on chest imaging): a focal consolidation with air bronchograms on chest radiograph in a febrile patient with productive cough confirms pneumonia and warrants antibiotics. Acute bronchitis is a clinical diagnosis without radiographic consolidation. A viral URI would not produce a focal infiltrate. Allergic rhinitis and chronic bronchitis exacerbation do not cause lobar consolidation on imaging.
  4. The correct answer is C (Cough and congestion commonly last up to two weeks): educating patients that viral URI symptoms, especially cough, can reasonably persist for ten to fourteen days reduces unnecessary antibiotic requests. Resolution within twenty-four hours or three days is unrealistic and sets false expectations. Duration beyond four days does not by itself indicate bacterial infection and should not trigger reflexive antibiotics.
  5. The correct answer is B (Limit use to no more than three consecutive days): topical decongestant sprays like oxymetazoline can cause rebound congestion, rhinitis medicamentosa, when used beyond three days. Two weeks of continuous use is well beyond the safe window. Combining agents indefinitely increases this risk rather than eliminating it. The rebound phenomenon is well documented, so claiming no risk is inaccurate.
  6. The correct answer is E (Fever, tonsillar exudate, tender adenopathy, and absent cough): these four features comprise the classic Centor criteria, and having all four yields the highest pretest probability of streptococcal pharyngitis warranting rapid testing. Cough and conjunctivitis are typically associated with viral causes rather than strep. Absent fever and mild erythema lower rather than raise suspicion. Age over forty-five is actually a negative Centor point, not a positive one.
  7. The correct answer is B (Send a confirmatory throat culture in this age group): a negative rapid antigen test in children and adolescents should be backed up with a throat culture because of the test's imperfect sensitivity, since missed strep can lead to complications. Treating empirically despite a negative result and moderate pretest probability is not recommended. A monospot is not the next step for suspected strep. Simply reassuring without culture confirmation risks missing true infection in this population.
  8. The correct answer is A (A 50-year-old with cough, no exudate, and mild fever): age over forty-five subtracts a point, and the presence of cough also subtracts a point, while absence of exudate means no point is gained there either, yielding a low overall score. The other options each accumulate multiple positive Centor features such as exudate, adenopathy, and absent cough. Lower scores correlate with lower probability of true strep infection. This patient is best managed with supportive care rather than testing.
  9. The correct answer is D (A standard five day course of oral azithromycin therapy): a macrolide is the preferred alternative in patients with a true anaphylactic penicillin allergy since cephalosporins carry cross-reactivity risk with severe IgE-mediated reactions. Cephalexin should be avoided in patients with anaphylactic penicillin allergy history. Reduced-dose amoxicillin is still a penicillin and inappropriate here. Doxycycline is not a recommended first-line agent for group A streptococcal pharyngitis.
  10. The correct answer is C (Refer urgently for drainage of suspected peritonsillar abscess): uvular deviation, trismus, and muffled voice are classic signs of peritonsillar abscess, which requires urgent drainage and cannot be managed with oral antibiotics alone. Waiting a week risks airway compromise and spread of infection. A rapid strep test does not address the surgical emergency at hand. Conservative gargling measures are inappropriate for a suppurative deep space infection.
  11. The correct answer is B (A double sickening pattern with worsening after initial improvement): a biphasic course where symptoms initially improve then worsen again is one of the strongest clinical indicators of secondary bacterial sinusitis. Short symptom duration under five days favors a viral cause. Mild congestion without facial pain and brief clear rhinorrhea are typical of uncomplicated viral URIs. Sneezing and itchy eyes without fever point toward allergic rather than infectious sinus disease.
  12. The correct answer is A (Fever of 39 C with purulent discharge for three consecutive days at onset): high fever with purulent discharge persisting for at least three consecutive days at the start of illness meets the severe-onset criterion for acute bacterial rhinosinusitis, independent of any biphasic pattern. The other choices describe mild, brief, or rapidly resolving symptoms consistent with an uncomplicated viral process rather than a severe bacterial onset.
  13. The correct answer is D (Amoxicillin clavulanate used as first-line combination antibiotic therapy today): current guidelines recommend amoxicillin clavulanate rather than amoxicillin alone as first-line therapy because of rising resistance among sinusitis pathogens. Azithromycin monotherapy is discouraged due to high rates of resistant Streptococcus pneumoniae. Fluoroquinolones are reserved for treatment failure or true penicillin allergy, not first-line use. Trimethoprim sulfamethoxazole alone also has unacceptably high resistance rates for this indication.
  14. The correct answer is C (Obtain emergent orbital and sinus imaging with urgent referral): proptosis and painful ophthalmoplegia in the setting of sinusitis suggest orbital cellulitis or abscess, a vision- and life-threatening complication requiring emergent imaging and specialist referral. Oral antibiotics alone are insufficient for this level of complication. Topical measures and reassurance are dangerously inadequate. This presentation is not an expected feature of routine sinusitis and needs urgent evaluation.
  15. The correct answer is D (An intranasal corticosteroid spray used for overall symptom relief): intranasal corticosteroids have modest evidence supporting symptomatic improvement when used alongside antibiotics for acute bacterial rhinosinusitis. Oral systemic steroids as monotherapy are not supported and are not a substitute for antibiotics when indicated. Antifungal prophylaxis has no role in typical bacterial sinusitis. Extended decongestant use risks rebound congestion and is not an evidence-based adjunct.
  16. The correct answer is B (Acute otitis media involving active middle ear infection): a bulging, erythematous tympanic membrane with reduced mobility on pneumatic otoscopy in a febrile, irritable child is the classic diagnostic triad for acute otitis media. Otitis externa involves the ear canal, not the middle ear, and typically causes pain with tragal traction. Otitis media with effusion lacks the acute bulging and erythema of active infection. Bullous myringitis without middle ear findings would not show bulging with decreased mobility.
  17. The correct answer is A (A healthy 2-year-old with mild unilateral pain and low fever): guidelines support observation with analgesics for children six months and older with unilateral, non-severe symptoms and shared decision making with caregivers. Infants under six months should receive antibiotics regardless of severity. Otorrhea or severe symptoms warrant immediate treatment rather than watchful waiting. Craniofacial anomalies or other risk factors also favor immediate antibiotic therapy over observation.
  18. The correct answer is D (High-dose amoxicillin at the maximum recommended pediatric dose): high-dose amoxicillin remains first-line for uncomplicated acute otitis media in children without recent antibiotic exposure or penicillin allergy, providing adequate coverage against resistant pneumococcus. Azithromycin has higher resistance rates among common otitis pathogens and is reserved for specific allergy situations. Clindamycin lacks reliable activity against Haemophilus influenzae. Trimethoprim sulfamethoxazole has poor activity against many otitis media pathogens and is not first-line.
  19. The correct answer is C (Continue observation since effusion commonly persists after treated infection): residual middle ear effusion is common for weeks to months after successfully treated acute otitis media and does not itself indicate treatment failure or require further antibiotics. Additional antibiotics are not indicated for asymptomatic effusion. Emergent surgical referral or imaging is unwarranted without symptoms. Indefinite prophylactic antibiotics are not appropriate for a single resolving episode.
  20. The correct answer is B (Acute mastoiditis with suppurative extension into the mastoid bone): postauricular swelling with erythema and outward protrusion of the ear alongside fever in a patient with otitis media is the classic presentation of acute mastoiditis, a suppurative complication requiring urgent evaluation. Cholesteatoma typically develops gradually and does not present with acute postauricular swelling. Simple otitis externa involves canal pain rather than postauricular swelling. Eustachian tube dysfunction causes fullness and hearing changes, not this acute presentation.
  21. The correct answer is A (Acute otitis externa related to recent water exposure): pain reproduced by tragal traction and auricular movement, with canal erythema and debris, is characteristic of otitis externa, often related to water exposure. Acute otitis media involves the middle ear and would show tympanic membrane changes rather than canal tenderness. Cholesteatoma is a chronic process rather than an acute painful presentation. Serous otitis media causes effusion without the tragal tenderness seen here.
  22. The correct answer is D (Topical antibiotic otic drops with or without steroid): topical antibiotic drops, often combined with a corticosteroid, are first-line for uncomplicated otitis externa and achieve high local drug concentrations without systemic exposure. Oral antibiotics are reserved for extension beyond the canal or immunocompromised patients. Systemic steroids alone do not treat the underlying infection. Simple observation is inappropriate for a painful active infection.
  23. The correct answer is B (Malignant otitis externa with early skull base extension): severe pain out of proportion to findings, granulation tissue at the bony cartilaginous junction, and diabetes strongly suggest malignant otitis externa, an invasive Pseudomonas infection that can extend to skull base osteomyelitis. Simple viral otitis externa does not cause granulation tissue or this severity of pain. Cerumen impaction does not cause purulent drainage or granulation tissue. Eustachian dysfunction and bullous myringitis do not present with canal granulation tissue.
  24. The correct answer is C (Using acidifying ear drops after water exposure to dry the canal): drying agents such as isopropyl alcohol and acetic acid mixtures after swimming help restore an acidic canal environment that discourages bacterial and fungal growth. Petroleum jelly inside the canal does not prevent moisture retention effectively and is not standard counseling. Cotton swabs can abrade the canal and push debris deeper, increasing infection risk. Avoiding all showering is impractical and unnecessary.
  25. The correct answer is A (Acute bronchitis without any focal consolidation on exam): a persistent cough without focal lung findings, fever, or signs of consolidation in an otherwise well patient is the classic clinical picture of acute bronchitis, a self-limited condition usually of viral origin. Pneumonia would typically show focal crackles, hypoxia, or abnormal vital signs. Sinusitis presents with facial pain and nasal symptoms, not isolated cough. Pulmonary embolism would present with acute dyspnea or pleuritic chest pain, not a gradual eight-day cough.
  26. The correct answer is D (Antibiotics are not routinely recommended even with purulent sputum): most acute bronchitis is viral, and sputum color alone does not indicate bacterial infection, so antibiotics are not routinely recommended regardless of sputum appearance. Evidence does not show a meaningful reduction in cough duration with antibiotics in healthy adults. Cough duration beyond five days is common in bronchitis and does not by itself justify antibiotics. Fever alone without evidence of pneumonia does not warrant antibiotic therapy.
  27. The correct answer is E (Simple antitussives or honey-based remedies used for symptom relief): symptomatic cough suppressants and honey-based preparations have modest evidence for relief in bronchitis-associated cough. Antibiotics do not meaningfully shorten cough duration in typical acute bronchitis. Inhaled or oral corticosteroids are not first-line for isolated bronchitis cough without wheeze. Bronchodilators are reserved for patients with documented wheeze or underlying reactive airway disease, not routine prophylaxis.
  28. The correct answer is B (Short course of a bronchodilator inhaler for wheeze): when bronchospasm and wheeze accompany acute bronchitis, a short course of a bronchodilator can provide symptomatic relief even without a formal asthma diagnosis. Antibiotics remain unnecessary since the underlying process is still typically viral. A high-dose steroid burst is not first-line for uncomplicated bronchitis with mild wheeze. Nebulized hypertonic saline is not standard therapy for this presentation.
  29. The correct answer is C (Focal crackles present along with fever and clear tachypnea): focal crackles combined with fever and tachypnea suggest a focal consolidative process and should prompt evaluation for pneumonia rather than a bronchitis diagnosis. A prolonged cough alone with clear lungs is consistent with bronchitis. Mild fatigue without systemic signs does not suggest pneumonia. A nonproductive nighttime cough is a nonspecific bronchitis symptom rather than a pneumonia red flag.
  30. The correct answer is A (Inpatient hospitalization with active consideration of intensive care admission): this patient scores four points on CURB-65 from confusion, elevated blood urea nitrogen, hypotension (systolic blood pressure under 90), and age 65 or older, since a respiratory rate of 24 does not reach the CURB-65 threshold of 30 or higher and does not itself score a point. A score of four indicates severe pneumonia that warrants hospitalization and evaluation for intensive care. Outpatient management is inappropriate at this score. A single antibiotic dose without admission does not address the severity indicated by this score.
  31. The correct answer is D (Blood urea nitrogen greater than 19 mg per dL): an elevated blood urea nitrogen above roughly 19 to 20 mg per dL is the U in CURB-65 and represents one of the five scoring criteria reflecting renal stress from severe infection. A systolic pressure above 100 is actually the threshold that does not score a point, since the criterion is hypotension below that level. A respiratory rate below 20 also does not score, since the criterion is tachypnea at or above 30. Age under 65 and normal mental status likewise do not contribute points.
  32. The correct answer is B (Outpatient oral antibiotics prescribed with close scheduled follow-up): a CURB-65 score of zero indicates low mortality risk and supports safe outpatient management with oral antibiotics and follow-up. Intensive care admission is reserved for high-severity scores, not a score of zero. General ward admission is unnecessary for low-risk patients who can be managed as outpatients. Emergency department intravenous antibiotics without discharge planning is not indicated for this low-risk presentation.
  33. The correct answer is C (Amoxicillin or doxycycline used as first-line monotherapy today): for a healthy outpatient without comorbidities, amoxicillin or doxycycline monotherapy is recommended first-line empiric therapy for community-acquired pneumonia. Broad-spectrum intravenous agents like vancomycin with piperacillin tazobactam are reserved for severe or hospital-associated infections. Meropenem is inappropriate first-line outpatient therapy given its broad spectrum and resistance concerns. Linezolid is not a standard first-line agent for typical outpatient pneumonia.
  34. The correct answer is A (A respiratory fluoroquinolone or a combination beta-lactam with macrolide): patients with comorbidities such as COPD and diabetes require broader empiric coverage, typically a respiratory fluoroquinolone or a beta-lactam combined with a macrolide, due to higher risk of resistant or atypical organisms. Monotherapy identical to a healthy patient underestimates the risk in this population. Low-dose amoxicillin monotherapy is insufficient coverage for comorbid patients. Withholding antibiotics is inappropriate for confirmed pneumonia regardless of comorbid stability.
  35. The correct answer is E (Most patients show clinical improvement within three to five days): most patients on appropriate antibiotics show measurable clinical improvement, such as decreasing fever, within three to five days, which is useful counseling for follow-up planning. Improvement within twenty-four hours is unrealistic to promise. Radiographic clearing lags well behind clinical improvement and often takes weeks, not forty-eight hours. Symptoms worsening for a full week despite appropriate therapy should prompt reassessment rather than being expected as normal.
  36. The correct answer is D (A short-acting bronchodilator with repeated dosing and close reassessment): repeated short-acting beta agonist dosing with close reassessment is the cornerstone of initial acute asthma exacerbation management. Long-acting beta agonists are not appropriate as monotherapy for acute rescue treatment and are unsafe without concurrent inhaled steroid. Antibiotics are not indicated unless there is clear evidence of bacterial infection. Immediate intubation without first trialing bronchodilator therapy is premature for a patient who is not in respiratory failure.
  37. The correct answer is B (Systemic corticosteroids started to reduce ongoing airway inflammation): systemic corticosteroids should be added early in moderate to severe exacerbations that do not fully respond to bronchodilators alone, since they reduce airway inflammation and prevent relapse. Long-acting muscarinic antagonists are maintenance agents, not acute rescue therapy. Inhaled antibiotics have no established role in typical asthma exacerbations. Sedatives are contraindicated in respiratory distress because they can worsen hypoventilation.
  38. The correct answer is C (An acute COPD exacerbation meeting standard clinical criteria): increased dyspnea, increased sputum volume, and purulent sputum color change are the classic Anthonisen criteria defining an acute COPD exacerbation. New-onset asthma would not occur in a patient with an established COPD diagnosis and this symptom pattern. A simple viral URI would not typically cause this degree of sputum volume and purulence change. Stable chronic bronchitis by definition lacks this acute worsening pattern.
  39. The correct answer is A (Presence of purulent sputum as one of the meeting criteria): purulent sputum as one of the two or more Anthonisen criteria met is the key factor supporting antibiotic therapy in a COPD exacerbation, since it suggests bacterial involvement. An unmeasured peak flow does not itself justify antibiotics. A remote history of pneumonia is not a current indicator for antibiotic use in this exacerbation. Normal resting oxygen saturation and unchanged exercise tolerance do not support the decision to add antibiotics.
  40. The correct answer is D (Short-acting bronchodilators, a short course of oral corticosteroids, and antibiotics if criteria met): the standard outpatient approach combines increased short-acting bronchodilator use, a brief oral corticosteroid course, and antibiotics only when appropriate criteria such as purulent sputum are met. A long-acting bronchodilator alone does not address the acute inflammatory component of an exacerbation. High-dose inhaled steroid alone without bronchodilator therapy is not standard acute management. Antibiotics without regard to sputum characteristics is not evidence based.
  41. The correct answer is B (New confusion or inability to maintain adequate oxygen saturation): altered mental status or failure to maintain adequate oxygenation despite supplemental oxygen are red flags indicating a severe exacerbation that requires hospitalization. A mild increase in baseline dyspnea previously managed at home is more consistent with outpatient care. Sputum color change alone without other severity markers does not mandate admission. An unchanged stable home oxygen requirement suggests the patient is not in acute severe distress.
  42. The correct answer is C (An intranasal corticosteroid spray used consistently once daily): intranasal corticosteroids are first-line therapy for moderate to severe or persistent allergic rhinitis given their superior efficacy on nasal congestion, sneezing, and itching. Continuous oral decongestant use carries cardiovascular risks and does not address the underlying allergic inflammation. Daily topical decongestant use risks rebound congestion with prolonged use. Oral systemic corticosteroids are not appropriate as routine first-line therapy given their side effect profile.
  43. The correct answer is E (A second-generation non-sedating oral antihistamine taken once daily): second-generation antihistamines such as cetirizine or loratadine relieve nasal and ocular itching with minimal sedation, making them suitable for someone operating machinery. First-generation antihistamines cause significant sedation and impair performance on tasks requiring alertness. Continuous or nightly decongestant spray risks rebound congestion and does not address itching. High-dose systemic corticosteroids are not appropriate first-line therapy for routine allergic rhinitis symptoms.
  44. The correct answer is A (An intranasal anticholinergic spray such as ipratropium used daily): intranasal ipratropium specifically targets watery rhinorrhea by blocking cholinergic-mediated glandular secretion and is a useful add-on when this symptom persists despite steroid therapy. An oral decongestant targets congestion rather than watery discharge specifically. A leukotriene antagonist is a weaker option overall and not targeted at rhinorrhea. A topical antihistamine addresses sneezing and itching more than isolated rhinorrhea.
  45. The correct answer is D (Referral for formal allergen immunotherapy evaluation and testing): when standard pharmacotherapy fails to adequately control allergic rhinitis, allergen immunotherapy offers a disease-modifying option through referral for allergy evaluation and testing. Escalating oral systemic corticosteroid doses long term carries significant adverse effects and is not appropriate maintenance therapy. Discontinuing all treatment abandons the patient without addressing ongoing symptoms. Long-term daily topical decongestant use causes rebound congestion and is never appropriate.
  46. The correct answer is B (Apply a topical vasoconstrictor and reattempt firm direct pressure): if initial compression fails, applying a topical vasoconstrictor such as oxymetazoline followed by continued firm direct pressure is the appropriate next conservative step before more invasive measures. Posterior packing is reserved for bleeding refractory to anterior measures or suspected posterior source. Discharging without further intervention is inappropriate for ongoing active bleeding. Surgical ligation is a last resort reserved for bleeding refractory to all conservative and packing measures.
  47. The correct answer is C (Chemical cauterization performed today with topical silver nitrate): silver nitrate chemical cauterization of a visible bleeding vessel in Kiesselbach's plexus is an effective in-office procedure for recurrent anterior epistaxis from an identifiable source. Reversing anticoagulation is only relevant if the patient is on such medication and is not a universal first step. Hospital admission is unnecessary for a localized, identifiable anterior bleeding source. Antibiotics and saline irrigation alone do not address the vascular source of recurrent bleeding.
  48. The correct answer is A (Easy bruising and gum bleeding along with epistaxis): a pattern of easy bruising, gum bleeding, and recurrent epistaxis together suggests a systemic bleeding tendency warranting coagulation workup. Nosebleeds limited to dry winter months are typically explained by mucosal irritation from low humidity. Epistaxis resolving quickly with brief pressure is reassuring and typical of benign anterior bleeding. Occasional bleeding after vigorous nose blowing is a common benign trigger, not a red flag for systemic disease.
  49. The correct answer is D (Infectious mononucleosis caused by primary Epstein-Barr virus infection): posterior cervical lymphadenopathy, splenomegaly, and prolonged fatigue with pharyngitis in a young adult strongly suggest infectious mononucleosis from Epstein-Barr virus. Streptococcal pharyngitis alone does not typically cause splenomegaly or posterior cervical adenopathy. A simple viral URI would not produce splenomegaly or this degree of prolonged fatigue. Acute bacterial sinusitis presents with facial pain and nasal symptoms, not splenomegaly.
  50. The correct answer is B (Avoid contact sports for at least three to four weeks from symptom onset): due to the risk of splenic rupture, contact sports and strenuous activity should be avoided for a minimum of three to four weeks from symptom onset, with return guided by clinical resolution. Returning after only three days is dangerously premature given ongoing splenic enlargement risk. No restriction at all ignores a well-documented rupture risk. Waiting until after rupture occurs defeats the purpose of preventive counseling.
  51. The correct answer is E (A diffuse maculopapular rash typically appearing several days later): administration of aminopenicillins such as amoxicillin in patients with infectious mononucleosis classically produces a diffuse maculopapular rash, which is not a true IgE-mediated allergy but a virus-drug interaction. Immediate anaphylaxis is not the typical pattern seen with this specific interaction. Severe hepatotoxicity and acute renal failure are not the classic reaction described. Airway angioedema is not the hallmark finding associated with this well-known amoxicillin-mono interaction.
  52. The correct answer is C (EBV-specific viral capsid antigen IgM antibody testing performed): heterophile antibody testing has lower sensitivity in young children, so EBV-specific serology such as viral capsid antigen IgM is more useful for confirming acute infection when the initial test is negative but suspicion remains high. A repeat complete blood count alone does not confirm the diagnosis, though atypical lymphocytosis is supportive. Random blood glucose has no diagnostic value for this condition. Erythrocyte sedimentation rate alone is nonspecific and does not confirm EBV infection.
  53. The correct answer is A (Croup of moderate severity based on today's exam): a barky cough with stridor at rest and mild retractions in a toddler, most common overnight, is the classic presentation of moderate croup from parainfluenza virus laryngotracheitis. Bacterial tracheitis typically presents with a toxic-appearing child and high fever, distinct from this picture. Foreign body aspiration usually has a sudden choking onset rather than a gradual barky cough. Epiglottitis presents with drooling, tripod positioning, and a toxic appearance, which are absent here.
  54. The correct answer is D (A single dose of oral or intramuscular dexamethasone): a single dose of dexamethasone is recommended for essentially all children with croup, including mild cases, because it reduces airway edema and decreases return visits and hospitalization. Antibiotics are not indicated since croup is viral in etiology. Long-acting bronchodilators are not part of standard croup treatment, which is not primarily a bronchospastic process. Antihistamines have no established role in reducing croup-related airway inflammation.
  55. The correct answer is B (Nebulized racemic epinephrine with a period of observation): severe croup with stridor at rest and significant respiratory distress warrants nebulized racemic epinephrine in addition to dexamethasone, followed by observation for rebound symptoms. Discharge home without treating the acute severe presentation is unsafe. Antibiotics are not indicated for this viral process. Tracheostomy is not indicated for typical severe croup responsive to medical therapy, and inhaled corticosteroid is not a substitute for the needed epinephrine and systemic steroid.
  56. The correct answer is C (Drooling with a preference to sit forward in a tripod position): drooling and a tripod sitting position reflect an inability to manage secretions and airway protection concerns classic for epiglottitis, a true emergency. A barky seal-like cough with hoarseness is characteristic of croup rather than epiglottitis. Gradual onset with rhinorrhea over days fits the typical viral course of croup. Nighttime worsening is a classic croup pattern, not a distinguishing feature of epiglottitis.
  57. The correct answer is A (Classic pertussis with the characteristic paroxysmal whooping cough): paroxysmal coughing fits with an inspiratory whoop and post-tussive vomiting persisting over weeks in an unvaccinated infant is the classic presentation of pertussis. Viral bronchiolitis typically presents with wheezing and respiratory distress in infants rather than this paroxysmal whooping pattern. Acute bronchitis does not typically cause the characteristic whoop or prolonged paroxysmal course. Croup presents with barky cough and stridor rather than paroxysmal whooping fits.
  58. The correct answer is D (Hospitalization with close monitoring for apnea and antibiotic therapy): infants under six months with pertussis are at high risk for apnea, hypoxia, and complications, so hospitalization with monitoring plus macrolide antibiotic therapy is recommended. Outpatient management with only a cough suppressant does not address the apnea risk or provide antimicrobial treatment. Reassurance alone is inappropriate given the documented risk of severe disease and death in this age group. Bronchodilator therapy is not the primary intervention for pertussis, which is not a bronchospastic disease.
  59. The correct answer is E (Postexposure macrolide antibiotic prophylaxis given to all close contacts): macrolide antibiotic prophylaxis is recommended for close contacts of a confirmed pertussis case, particularly when a vulnerable newborn is in the household, regardless of the contacts' own vaccination status. Doing nothing for asymptomatic contacts ignores the transmission risk to a vulnerable infant. Isolation alone without prophylaxis does not adequately reduce the risk of secondary transmission that has already begun. Vaccination alone does not provide the immediate protection prophylactic antibiotics offer, and inhaled corticosteroids have no role in preventing pertussis transmission.
  60. The correct answer is B (Recommend supportive care with fluids, rest, and analgesics): uncomplicated viral upper respiratory infection is self-limited and antibiotics do not shorten its course or prevent complications. Empiric antibiotics are inappropriate without evidence of bacterial infection and contribute to resistance. A chest radiograph is not indicated without exam findings suggesting pneumonia. Antivirals are reserved for influenza within the appropriate treatment window and confirmed or highly suspected diagnosis.
  61. The correct answer is D (Perform a rapid strep antigen test before treating): this patient has four Centor criteria (fever, exudates, tender adenopathy, absence of cough), placing her in a high-probability group where testing before antibiotics is the recommended approach. Treating empirically without confirmation risks unnecessary antibiotic exposure. Reassurance alone ignores a meaningfully elevated pretest probability of group A strep. A monospot is only warranted if strep testing is negative and mononucleosis is suspected.
  62. The correct answer is C (Double worsening after initial improvement of symptoms): a biphasic course, where symptoms improve then worsen again, is one of the classic clinical markers suggesting secondary bacterial sinusitis rather than a prolonged viral course. Persistent symptoms beyond ten days without improvement is another accepted marker, but five days alone is too short and typical of viral illness. Isolated headache or early clear rhinorrhea are nonspecific viral features. Mild congestion without facial pain does not meet criteria for bacterial sinusitis.
  63. The correct answer is A (Amoxicillin-clavulanate at an appropriate weight-based dose): current guidelines recommend amoxicillin-clavulanate as first-line therapy for acute bacterial rhinosinusitis because it covers the typical pathogens including beta-lactamase producing strains. Fluoroquinolones are reserved for treatment failures or significant allergy given resistance and toxicity concerns. Trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole has rising resistance among common sinus pathogens. Clindamycin lacks reliable coverage against Haemophilus influenzae, a key causative organism.
  64. The correct answer is B (Start oral amoxicillin now given severe symptoms and bulging membrane): guidelines support immediate antibiotic therapy for acute otitis media with severe symptoms such as bulging membrane, marked otalgia, or high fever rather than observation. Watchful waiting is an option only for mild, non-severe cases in appropriate age groups. Topical drops treat otitis externa, not middle ear infection behind an intact drum. Tympanocentesis is reserved for treatment failure or immunocompromised patients, not initial management.
  65. The correct answer is C (Community-acquired pneumonia with right lower lobe consolidation): the clinical picture of fever, productive cough, pleuritic pain, and a focal consolidation described on imaging is classic for community-acquired pneumonia. COPD exacerbation does not typically produce a new focal consolidation on imaging. Pneumothorax would show absent lung markings and possible shift, not consolidation. Heart failure typically shows bilateral effusions and cardiomegaly rather than a unilateral consolidative pattern.
  66. The correct answer is A (Hospital admission given a high CURB-65 score): this patient meets confusion, elevated BUN, elevated respiratory rate, and low blood pressure criteria for a CURB-65 score of at least four, indicating severe pneumonia requiring hospital admission, often with consideration of intensive care. Outpatient management is appropriate only for low CURB-65 scores of zero or one. Discharge home would place this unstable patient at significant risk. Urgent care lacks the resources needed for this severity of illness.
  67. The correct answer is C (Acute bronchitis, managed with supportive care, not antibiotics): prolonged cough without fever, focal findings, or signs of consolidation in an otherwise well patient is typical of acute bronchitis, which is usually viral and does not benefit from antibiotics. Bacterial pneumonia would be expected to show fever or abnormal lung findings. Tuberculosis is unlikely without risk factors, weight loss, or night sweats. Pulmonary embolism would present with different features such as pleuritic pain, hypoxia, or risk factors, not isolated prolonged cough.
  68. The correct answer is E (Inhaled short-acting beta agonist with systemic corticosteroids): moderate asthma exacerbation with a peak flow around 50 to 70 percent of personal best requires repeated short-acting bronchodilator dosing plus systemic corticosteroids to reduce airway inflammation and prevent relapse. Inhaled corticosteroids alone act too slowly for acute symptom relief. Montelukast has no role in acute exacerbation management. Long-acting beta agonists are never used as monotherapy and are not appropriate for acute rescue therapy.
  69. The correct answer is B (Bronchodilators, a short course of oral corticosteroids, and antibiotics): a COPD exacerbation with increased sputum purulence, volume, and dyspnea meets criteria for antibiotic therapy in addition to bronchodilators and a short corticosteroid course, which reduces treatment failure and shortens recovery. Bronchodilators alone would undertreat the inflammatory component and possible bacterial trigger. Chest tube placement is not indicated without evidence of pneumothorax. Anticoagulation is inappropriate without clinical suspicion for embolism.
  70. The correct answer is A (Intranasal corticosteroid spray used regularly during symptomatic season): seasonal allergic rhinitis with pale boggy turbinates and classic pollen-triggered symptoms responds best to regular intranasal corticosteroids, which are the most effective single therapy for nasal symptoms. Antibiotics are not indicated without evidence of bacterial infection. Systemic steroid injections carry unnecessary systemic risk for a condition manageable topically. Decongestant sprays used beyond a few days risk rebound congestion, and saline alone is only adjunctive.
  71. Posterior packing and surgical ligation are reserved for bleeds refractory to conservative measures.   72. The correct answer is A (Heterophile antibody test, also known as a monospot test): posterior cervical adenopathy, splenomegaly, and a negative strep test in a young adult strongly suggest infectious mononucleosis, which the heterophile antibody test can confirm. Immediate return risks splenic injury from even minor trauma. No restriction ignores a real, if uncommon, risk. Indefinite restriction without reassessment is unnecessarily prolonged once splenomegaly resolves.
  74. The statement about absence lowering the score describes the reverse of the correct relationship.

  131.   132. Hospitalization for intravenous antibiotics is reserved for systemic toxicity or extensive infection, neither present here.   133. The correct answer is B (Beta blockers): Beta blockers are well documented to trigger or worsen psoriasis flares and should be considered as a potential culprit when psoriasis worsens after starting new antihypertensive therapy.   134.   135. Mupirocin is a topical antibiotic used for bacterial skin infections, not viral eruptions.
