Darier sign and Dennie-Morgan fold are associated with mastocytosis and atopic dermatitis respectively, not psoriasis.
  197. The correct answer is D (Psoriatic arthritis affecting the distal interphalangeal joints and nail matrix): nail pitting and onycholysis in a patient with known psoriasis, combined with new joint stiffness, should raise concern for psoriatic arthritis, which affects up to a third of patients with psoriasis and often involves the distal interphalangeal joints along with nail changes. Onychomycosis alone would not explain the new joint symptoms. Rheumatoid arthritis classically spares the distal interphalangeal joints and is not linked to psoriatic nail changes. Reactive arthritis follows a specific infectious trigger rather than arising in the setting of chronic psoriasis.
  198. The correct answer is A (Refer for consideration of oral isotretinoin therapy given her disease severity): severe nodulocystic acne that has failed topical therapy and carries a significant risk of permanent scarring is an indication for oral isotretinoin, typically after dermatology involvement given its teratogenicity and monitoring requirements. Continuing benzoyl peroxide alone has already proven inadequate for this severity of disease. A topical retinoid alone is reasonable for mild to moderate acne but is insufficient for scarring nodulocystic lesions. Increased facial washing does not address the follicular inflammation driving nodulocystic acne and can worsen irritation.
  199. The correct answer is B (Topical retinoid used with or without added benzoyl peroxide therapy): mild comedonal acne with only occasional inflammatory papules is appropriately managed first-line with a topical retinoid, often combined with benzoyl peroxide, which targets both comedone formation and Cutibacterium acnes. Oral isotretinoin is reserved for severe nodulocystic or scarring acne unresponsive to conventional therapy. Systemic corticosteroids are not standard acne therapy and can actually worsen the condition. Oral spironolactone is used for hormonally driven acne in adult women, not typically as a first choice in an adolescent male.
  200. The correct answer is E (Septate branching hyphae crossing the epidermal cells on the slide): an annular plaque with central clearing and an active scaly border is classic for tinea corporis, and a positive KOH preparation showing septate branching hyphae confirms a dermatophyte infection. Pseudohyphae and budding yeast suggest a Candida infection rather than a dermatophyte. The spaghetti and meatballs pattern is characteristic of tinea versicolor caused by Malassezia, not tinea corporis. A Tzanck smear with multinucleated giant cells diagnoses herpes infections, not fungal disease, and acantholytic cells suggest a blistering autoimmune disorder instead.
  201. The correct answer is C (Microsporum species, a classic cause of fluorescent tinea capitis infection): blue-green fluorescence under Wood's lamp in tinea capitis is classically associated with Microsporum species, whereas Trichophyton tonsurans, now the most common cause of tinea capitis in North America, typically does not fluoresce. Malassezia furfur causes tinea versicolor on the trunk with a different clinical picture and no scalp hair loss. Candida albicans causes intertriginous infections in moist skin folds rather than scaling patchy alopecia of the scalp.
  202. The correct answer is C (Tinea versicolor caused by overgrowth of the Malassezia yeast species): hypopigmented or hyperpigmented finely scaling patches on the trunk that become more apparent after tanning, with the classic spaghetti and meatballs pattern of short hyphae and spores on KOH, confirm tinea versicolor. Vitiligo produces depigmented patches without scale and a negative KOH preparation. Pityriasis rosea presents with a herald patch followed by a Christmas tree distribution of oval plaques, not this KOH pattern. Tinea corporis presents as annular lesions with central clearing rather than the truncal hypopigmented patches described here.
  203. The correct answer is A (Permethrin 5 percent cream applied to the entire body from the neck down): burrows in the web spaces and wrists with intense nocturnal pruritus affecting multiple household members is classic scabies, and first-line treatment is permethrin cream left on for 8 to 14 hours, with treatment of all close contacts simultaneously to prevent reinfestation. Topical corticosteroids and antihistamines may help symptomatic itching but do not eradicate the mites. Topical antifungals have no activity against scabies mites. Scabies does not self-resolve and can spread further without treatment being given.
  204. The correct answer is B (Postscabetic pruritus and nodules from a residual hypersensitivity reaction to antigens): itchy nodules, particularly on the genitals and axillae, can persist for weeks after successful scabies eradication due to a residual hypersensitivity reaction to mite antigens, and this does not indicate treatment failure if no new burrows are present. Immediate retreatment is not necessary without evidence of active infestation such as fresh burrows. A new fungal infection or contact dermatitis to permethrin would not typically produce this same nodular distribution. Scalp involvement is a consideration mainly in infants and immunocompromised patients, not typical adults.
  205. The correct answer is B (Herpes zoster reactivating along a single thoracic sensory dermatome): a unilateral, dermatomal, grouped vesicular eruption on an erythematous base, preceded by localized burning or neuropathic pain, is the classic presentation of herpes zoster from reactivation of latent varicella zoster virus. Contact dermatitis would not respect a strict dermatomal boundary and typically corresponds to an area of direct exposure. Impetigo produces honey-colored crusted lesions rather than grouped vesicles in a dermatomal pattern. Bullous pemphigoid causes tense bullae in a more generalized distribution, not a single dermatome.
  206. The correct answer is D (Involvement of the tip or side of the nose, known as Hutchinson sign): vesicular involvement of the tip or side of the nose, innervated by the nasociliary branch of the trigeminal nerve, is called Hutchinson sign and predicts a significantly increased risk of ocular involvement in herpes zoster ophthalmicus, warranting urgent ophthalmology referral. Forehead involvement alone without nasal tip lesions carries lower specific risk for ocular disease. Distant cheek erythema and isolated scalp involvement do not indicate nasociliary nerve involvement. A rash presenting late and already resolving is less concerning than an early, active Hutchinson sign.
  207. The correct answer is A (Initiate oral antiviral therapy such as valacyclovir as promptly as possible): antiviral therapy is most effective when started within 72 hours of rash onset, and this patient at 60 hours still falls within that window, so prompt initiation of valacyclovir or a similar agent is appropriate to shorten disease duration and potentially reduce postherpetic neuralgia risk. Withholding treatment is incorrect since the window has not yet closed. Topical corticosteroids are not appropriate for treating the underlying viral infection. Antibiotics would only be indicated if secondary bacterial superinfection actually developed, not as initial therapy.
  208. The correct answer is B (Full-thickness excisional biopsy of the lesion performed with narrow margins): a lesion with asymmetry, irregular borders, color variation, diameter over 6 millimeters, and evolution over time meets ABCDE criteria concerning for melanoma, and the preferred diagnostic approach is a full-thickness excisional biopsy with narrow margins to allow accurate Breslow depth measurement. A shave biopsy risks transecting the base of the lesion and compromising staging if melanoma is confirmed. Reassurance and delayed follow-up is inappropriate given the concerning evolution described. Destructive treatments without tissue diagnosis would prevent histologic confirmation and staging entirely.
  209. The correct answer is E (Basal cell carcinoma presenting as a classic pearly nodule with telangiectasias): a pearly, translucent papule with rolled, telangiectatic borders and slow growth is the classic description of nodular basal cell carcinoma, the most common form of skin cancer. Squamous cell carcinoma more often presents as a scaly, crusted, or ulcerated hyperkeratotic papule or plaque rather than a pearly nodule. Melanoma presents with pigmented, irregular lesions rather than this translucent pearly appearance. Seborrheic keratosis and dermatofibroma have distinctly different textures and behavior that do not match this description at all.
  210. The correct answer is C (Squamous cell carcinoma with confirmed invasion into the underlying dermis): a tender, scaly, erythematous, bleeding papule that has grown over months in a sun-exposed area, with biopsy showing atypical keratinocytes invading the dermis, confirms invasive squamous cell carcinoma rather than a precancerous lesion. Actinic keratosis is a precursor lesion confined to the epidermis without dermal invasion on biopsy. Seborrheic keratosis is a benign, waxy, stuck-on lesion without malignant histology. Basal cell carcinoma typically shows a pearly, translucent appearance and a different histologic growth pattern altogether.
  211. The correct answer is D (Primary closure is appropriate given the relatively short time since the injury): clean lacerations presenting within the golden period, generally up to 6 to 8 hours on the body and longer on the face, with no signs of infection or significant contamination, are appropriate for primary closure. Leaving the wound open for secondary intention is reserved for heavily contaminated, infected, or bite wounds. Mandating a delay of seven days is unnecessary for a clean wound presenting promptly. Primary closure is not contraindicated on extremities in general based on anatomic location alone.
  212. The correct answer is E (Flexor tendon injury; refer promptly for surgical exploration and formal repair): inability to flex the distal interphalangeal joint after a deep palmar laceration indicates injury to the flexor digitorum profundus tendon, which requires surgical exploration and repair since these injuries do not heal adequately without operative intervention. A digital artery injury would cause bleeding or perfusion changes rather than a specific flexion deficit. An extensor tendon injury would impair extension, not flexion, of the digit. Treating this as a simple laceration without addressing the tendon injury would result in permanent loss of function.
  213. The correct answer is D (Administer tetanus toxoid vaccine alone without any tetanus immune globulin given): for a dirty or contaminated wound in a patient with a documented prior vaccination series more than 5 years since the last dose, tetanus toxoid vaccine alone is indicated; immune globulin is reserved for patients with unknown or incomplete vaccination status. Withholding prophylaxis entirely would leave the patient inadequately protected given the contaminated puncture wound and the 12 year interval. Immune globulin without vaccine would not provide the ongoing protection needed in this previously vaccinated patient.
  214. The correct answer is C (Leave the wound open with delayed closure and begin prophylactic antibiotic therapy): cat bites carry a high infection risk due to deep puncture wounds and Pasteurella multocida, so they are generally left open or undergo delayed primary closure along with prophylactic antibiotics such as amoxicillin-clavulanate, especially when early erythema is already present. Primary closure of a high-risk bite wound with early infection signs increases the risk of abscess formation under a closed wound. Cat bites do require careful management given their high infection rate. An occlusive dressing without antibiotics ignores the significant infection risk of feline punctures.
  215. The correct answer is A (Rest, ice, compression, elevation, and progressive functional rehabilitation exercises): a grade I to II lateral ankle sprain involving the anterior talofibular ligament, without bony tenderness meeting Ottawa criteria and with preserved weight-bearing, is managed conservatively with rest, ice, compression, elevation, and early functional rehabilitation rather than imaging or immobilization. Surgical reconstruction is reserved for severe grade III injuries or chronic instability, not an acute low-grade sprain. Long leg casting and prolonged non-weight-bearing are excessive for this injury pattern. Emergent MRI is unnecessary since the Ottawa Ankle Rules are not met here.
  216. The correct answer is B (Acute compartment syndrome requiring urgent pressure measurement and possible fasciotomy): severe pain out of proportion to the injury, tense swelling, and pain with passive stretch of the toes after a direct traumatic blow are hallmark signs of acute compartment syndrome, a surgical emergency requiring urgent compartment pressure measurement and possible fasciotomy to prevent irreversible damage. A simple contusion would not cause pain out of proportion or pain with passive stretch. Chronic exertional compartment syndrome develops gradually with exercise, unlike this acute presentation. Cellulitis and abrasion would not explain the tense compartment described.
  217. The correct answer is D (Achilles tendon rupture confirmed by the palpable gap and positive Thompson test): a palpable tendon defect combined with a positive Thompson test, in which squeezing the calf fails to produce plantarflexion, confirms a complete Achilles tendon rupture. A gastrocnemius strain would not produce a palpable tendon gap or an absent Thompson response. Deep vein thrombosis presents with calf swelling and tenderness but no palpable tendon defect or abnormal Thompson test. A ruptured Baker cyst causes posterior knee and calf swelling that can mimic deep vein thrombosis but does not produce a palpable Achilles gap.
  218. The correct answer is A (S1 nerve root, typically causing weak plantarflexion and lateral foot numbness): radiating pain down the posterior thigh and calf with lateral foot sensory loss and weak plantarflexion or a diminished ankle reflex is the classic pattern of S1 radiculopathy. L4 radiculopathy typically causes weak knee extension, medial leg sensory loss, and a diminished patellar reflex instead. L3 involvement affects the anterior thigh and hip flexion rather than the posterior leg and foot. L1 radiculopathy affects the groin and upper thigh and does not produce plantarflexion weakness or lateral foot numbness.
  219. The correct answer is C (Obtain plain radiographs and consider MRI to evaluate for metastatic disease): night pain unrelieved by rest, unintentional weight loss, and a history of malignancy are red flags for spinal metastasis, warranting prompt imaging rather than watchful waiting. Reassurance and continued conservative therapy without imaging would delay diagnosis of a potentially serious underlying malignancy. Physical therapy alone does not address the need to rule out a structural or malignant cause given these red flags. Muscle relaxants may provide symptomatic relief but do not substitute for appropriate imaging workup here.
  220. The correct answer is E (Staphylococcus aureus, for which wound culture with resistance testing should guide therapy): fluctuant skin abscesses clustering among close-contact athletes such as wrestlers are classically caused by Staphylococcus aureus, including community-acquired methicillin-resistant strains, so wound culture with sensitivity testing should guide antibiotic selection after drainage. Streptococcus pyogenes causes cellulitis and pharyngitis more than fluctuant abscesses in this contact-sport pattern. Candida and Pseudomonas are not typical causes of contact-sport skin abscess outbreaks. Herpes simplex causes vesicular lesions such as herpes gladiatorum, not a fluctuant abscess.
  221. The correct answer is B (Lymphangitic spread of infection tracking along superficial lymphatic channels): a tender red streak tracking proximally from an infected wound toward regional lymph nodes, accompanied by tender lymphadenopathy, represents lymphangitis, indicating spreading infection along lymphatic channels that requires prompt systemic antibiotic therapy. Superficial thrombophlebitis would present as a palpable cord along a vein rather than a linear streak tracking from an infected wound. An allergic reaction to topical ointment would cause diffuse dermatitis rather than a linear streak with lymphadenopathy. Bruising would not be linear, tender, or associated with node tenderness.
  222. The correct answer is A (Clean the wound with soap and water and keep it covered and moist): gentle cleaning with soap and water followed by a moist, covered wound environment promotes faster epithelialization and reduces infection risk for minor abrasions. Hydrogen peroxide and rubbing alcohol are cytotoxic to healthy tissue and delay healing when applied repeatedly to open wounds. Leaving a wound completely uncovered increases the risk of contamination and does not improve healing compared to a properly maintained moist covered environment over time.
  223. The correct answer is C (Cloudy fluid demonstrating a markedly elevated white cell count and positive Gram stain): cloudy, purulent-appearing fluid with a markedly elevated white cell count and a positive Gram stain indicates septic bursitis requiring antibiotics and possible surgical drainage. Clear, straw-colored fluid with a low white count is consistent with simple traumatic or friction-related bursitis rather than infection. Blood-tinged fluid alone is common after trauma and does not by itself indicate infection. A normal glucose level similar to serum glucose is reassuring against infection, since infected fluid typically shows a reduced glucose relative to serum.
  224. The correct answer is D (Absence of fever with a synovial white count in the low thousands range): an osteoarthritis flare typically produces a mildly inflammatory effusion with a white cell count in the low thousands and no fever, whereas septic arthritis usually produces marked systemic symptoms and a synovial white cell count often exceeding 50,000 cells per microliter. Joint effusion, joint line tenderness, pain with weight bearing, and crepitus can all occur with osteoarthritis alone and do not reliably distinguish it from infection, making synovial fluid analysis and clinical context the key differentiating factors here.
  225. The correct answer is B (Streptococcus pyogenes causing erysipelas of the superficial dermis and lymphatics): a sharply demarcated, raised-border, erythematous facial plaque with fever and chills is the classic presentation of erysipelas, most commonly caused by Streptococcus pyogenes, which involves the superficial dermis and lymphatics. Staphylococcus aureus folliculitis presents with small follicular pustules rather than a sharply demarcated plaque with systemic symptoms. Seborrheic dermatitis is a chronic, greasy, scaling condition without acute fever or sharp demarcation. Herpes simplex produces grouped vesicles rather than a diffuse sharply bordered plaque.
  226. The correct answer is A (Thoroughly irrigate, debride devitalized tissue, and consider delayed primary closure with antibiotics): a heavily contaminated wound presenting outside the ideal closure window with early signs of infection should be thoroughly irrigated, debrided of devitalized tissue, and managed with delayed primary closure along with antibiotics rather than immediate suturing. Closing a contaminated, already inflamed wound primarily risks trapping bacteria and precipitating an abscess. Simply covering the wound without cleaning ignores the significant contamination and infection risk. Suturing without irrigation would seal contaminants and devitalized tissue inside the wound.
  227. The correct answer is E (Routine reassurance along with continued skin self-examination and annual skin checks): a small, symmetric, uniformly colored lesion with regular borders that has remained stable over years is reassuring for a benign nevus and does not meet ABCDE criteria for melanoma, so routine reassurance with ongoing surveillance is appropriate. Urgent excisional biopsy is not warranted based solely on another person's subjective concern without objective worrisome features. Wide local excision with sentinel node biopsy is a treatment for confirmed invasive melanoma, not an undiagnosed benign-appearing lesion. Empiric destructive treatments without a tissue diagnosis are inappropriate here.
  228. The correct answer is B (Recurrent herpes simplex virus type 1 reactivation along the trigeminal nerve): recurrent, prodromal tingling followed by grouped vesicular lesions on the lip, triggered by sun exposure or stress, is the classic pattern of recurrent orolabial herpes simplex virus type 1 reactivation. Bacterial impetigo produces honey-colored crusted lesions rather than recurrent prodromal vesicles tied to specific triggers. Contact dermatitis to lip balm would correlate with product use rather than stress or sun exposure and would lack a viral prodrome. Aphthous stomatitis involves intraoral ulcers, and angular cheilitis affects the mouth corners with fissuring rather than vesicles.
  229. The correct answer is D (Reducing his alcohol intake along with his consumption of purine-rich foods): alcohol, particularly beer, and purine-rich foods such as red meat and organ meats are well-established dietary triggers for gout flares, so reducing their intake is a key lifestyle modification alongside appropriate pharmacologic therapy. Increasing red meat intake would worsen purine load and increase flare risk rather than help. Vitamin C has only a modest, secondary role in urate lowering and is not the primary recommended intervention. Fructose-containing beverages actually raise uric acid levels and would worsen gout control rather than improve it.
  230. The correct answer is C (Perform arthrocentesis with Gram stain, culture, and crystal analysis before starting treatment): a history of gout does not exclude septic arthritis, and a patient with fever and an inability to bear weight requires arthrocentesis with Gram stain, culture, and crystal analysis before assuming this is simply another gout flare, since untreated septic arthritis can rapidly destroy a joint. Assuming gout without aspiration risks missing a joint infection. Empiric antibiotics without first obtaining synovial fluid culture can obscure organism identification. A serum uric acid level can be normal or elevated in both gout and unrelated conditions and cannot reliably distinguish the two.
  231. The correct answer is E (Seborrheic dermatitis affecting sebaceous-gland-rich areas of the face and scalp): greasy, yellowish, scaling patches localized to sebaceous-gland-rich areas such as the eyebrows, nasolabial folds, and scalp margin are characteristic of seborrheic dermatitis, associated with Malassezia yeast overgrowth. Allergic contact dermatitis would typically correspond to a specific area of allergen exposure rather than these classic sites. Tinea faciei would show an annular, KOH-positive scaling patch rather than this greasy distribution. Psoriasis classically produces thick silvery scale on extensor surfaces, and rosacea presents with flushing and papulopustules, neither matching this description.
  232. The correct answer is A (Hidradenitis suppurativa affecting the axillary and inguinal intertriginous regions): recurrent, deep, interconnected painful nodules and abscesses with malodorous drainage and scarring in intertriginous areas such as the axillae and groin over more than a year is characteristic of hidradenitis suppurativa, a chronic inflammatory follicular disease. Simple furunculosis typically presents as isolated, self-limited boils rather than chronic interconnected sinus tracts with scarring. Tinea cruris presents as an annular, scaly, itchy rash rather than deep painful nodules with drainage. Contact dermatitis to deodorant would cause diffuse erythema without the deep nodules and chronic scarring described.
  233. The correct answer is D (Avoiding shaving the eyebrow entirely and trimming hair away from the edge only if truly necessary): eyebrow hair should never be shaved before laceration repair because it may not regrow properly and serves as an important landmark for precise wound edge alignment, so only minimal trimming should be performed if needed. Shaving the eyebrow risks permanent cosmetic disfigurement and loss of an alignment landmark. Avoiding local anesthesia would cause unnecessary pain and is not a recommended shortcut. Unnecessarily delaying closure of a clean facial wound increases infection risk and worsens the cosmetic outcome given the excellent blood supply of the face.
  234. The correct answer is C (Administer intravenous antibiotics, cover the wound, and arrange urgent orthopedic surgical evaluation): an open fracture with visible bone through the skin requires prompt intravenous antibiotics, sterile wound covering, tetanus assessment, and urgent orthopedic evaluation for surgical debridement and fixation to reduce infection and nonunion risk, regardless of intact distal pulses and sensation. Elective outpatient follow-up is inappropriate for an open fracture, which is a surgical emergency. Attempting closed reduction without antibiotics or surgical involvement ignores the significant contamination risk of an open wound. Discharging him home is unsafe given the high risk of osteomyelitis.
  235. The correct answer is B (Acute cholecystitis from gallbladder inflammation and obstruction): fatty food intolerance, RUQ pain radiating to the scapula, fever, and a positive Murphy sign are classic for acute cholecystitis. Pancreatitis typically causes epigastric pain radiating to the back, not the shoulder, and Murphy sign is not expected. Perforated ulcer causes sudden diffuse peritonitis rather than a focal positive Murphy sign. Pneumonia can cause referred abdominal pain but would show respiratory symptoms and would not produce Murphy sign.
  236. The correct answer is D (Acute diverticulitis with localized colonic inflammation): left lower quadrant pain with fever and altered bowel habits in an older adult is the classic presentation of diverticulitis, most common in the sigmoid colon. Mesenteric ischemia usually causes pain out of proportion to exam findings in a patient with vascular risk factors. Irritable bowel syndrome does not cause fever or true localized inflammation. Ischemic colitis typically affects the splenic flexure and presents with bloody diarrhea rather than isolated LLQ tenderness.
  237. The correct answer is A (Acute pyelonephritis with upper urinary tract infection): fever, flank pain, and nausea combined with pyuria, bacteriuria, and a positive nitrite indicate infection has ascended to the kidney, not just the bladder. Uncomplicated cystitis causes dysuria and urgency without fever or flank pain. Vaginitis would show squamous epithelial cells and would not explain fever or flank pain. Interstitial cystitis produces sterile urine without bacteria or nitrites, unlike this infected specimen.
  238. The correct answer is C (Non-contrast CT of the abdomen and pelvis): this is the most sensitive and specific study for detecting ureteral stones and remains the standard initial imaging for suspected nephrolithiasis in a stable adult. Ultrasound is useful, especially in pregnancy, but has lower sensitivity for smaller or ureteral stones. MRI is reserved for pregnant patients or when radiation must be avoided and ultrasound is inconclusive. Plain x-ray only detects radiopaque stones and misses many uric acid stones.
  239. The correct answer is B (Absence of blood or mucus in the stool): viral gastroenteritis typically causes watery, non-bloody diarrhea, while bacterial pathogens like Shigella or Campylobacter more often cause bloody or mucus-containing stool. High fever can occur with either but is more suggestive of an invasive bacterial process. Symptom onset within two hours suggests a preformed toxin such as Staphylococcus aureus, not a typical viral incubation. Localized right lower quadrant tenderness would raise concern for appendicitis rather than gastroenteritis.
  240. The correct answer is E (Severe dehydration requiring urgent intravenous fluid resuscitation): lethargy, sunken eyes, delayed capillary refill, and absent tears together indicate severe dehydration, typically ten percent or more of body weight loss, and warrant immediate IV rehydration rather than oral therapy alone. Mild dehydration presents with only slightly dry mucous membranes and normal mental status. Moderate dehydration shows some signs like decreased tears without lethargy or delayed capillary refill. Waiting to reassess with oral fluids alone would be unsafe given the depressed mental status.
  241. The correct answer is A (Elevated serum calcium suggesting possible hyperparathyroidism): recurrent calcium-based kidney stones with hypercalcemia should prompt evaluation for primary hyperparathyroidism, a correctable cause of stone recurrence. Elevated potassium is not a recognized cause of stone formation and would suggest a different problem. Low sodium reflecting dehydration does not explain recurrent stones and is not shown here. A normal bicarbonate does not rule out other metabolic contributors like hypercalciuria or hyperuricosuria that require urine testing, not just serum studies.
  242. The correct answer is D (Prompt upper endoscopy to evaluate for malignancy or stricture): weight loss, dysphagia, and hematemesis are alarm symptoms in a patient with reflux that require endoscopy to exclude esophageal cancer, stricture, or a bleeding ulcer. Empiric antacid trials are appropriate for uncomplicated heartburn without alarm features, not this presentation. Simply increasing acid suppression delays diagnosis of a potentially serious underlying cause. Reassurance is inappropriate once red-flag symptoms like bleeding and dysphagia are present.
  243. The correct answer is C (Biopsy-based testing obtained during the endoscopy already performed): since endoscopy was already done, biopsy urease testing or histology can be obtained directly and is not falsely lowered by PPI use in the same way breath and stool tests can be. Serum antibody testing cannot distinguish active from past infection and is not preferred. Urea breath and stool antigen tests have reduced sensitivity if the patient is still on a PPI and ideally require stopping it for one to two weeks first.
  244. The correct answer is B (Rebound tenderness and guarding at McBurney point): localized rebound and guarding over McBurney point, combined with the classic periumbilical-to-RLQ pain migration, strongly supports appendicitis with possible localized peritoneal irritation. Relief with flatus suggests a functional or obstructive process rather than appendiceal inflammation. Diffuse rigidity from the very onset would be atypical and more concerning for early diffuse peritonitis or perforation. Pain unrelated to eating and symmetric four-quadrant tenderness are not characteristic of appendicitis, which is typically focal.
  245. The correct answer is D (Oral doxycycline one hundred milligrams twice daily for seven days): doxycycline for seven days is now the preferred first-line treatment for chlamydia due to higher efficacy compared to single-dose azithromycin. Azithromycin is now reserved for situations where adherence to a multi-day course is a concern. Ceftriaxone alone treats gonorrhea, not chlamydia, and would leave this infection untreated. Watchful waiting is inappropriate because asymptomatic chlamydia still requires treatment to prevent complications and further transmission.
  246. The correct answer is A (Pelvic inflammatory disease requiring prompt antibiotic treatment): cervical motion tenderness plus adnexal tenderness in a sexually active woman with lower abdominal pain meets the minimum criteria for presumptive PID treatment, since delayed therapy risks infertility and abscess. Ovarian torsion typically causes sudden severe unilateral pain without cervical motion tenderness as the defining feature. A simple UTI would not cause cervical motion or adnexal tenderness. Endometriosis is a chronic condition and does not explain acute fever with these exam findings.
  247. The correct answer is C (Bacterial vaginosis from disrupted normal vaginal flora): a fishy odor, thin gray discharge, elevated pH above 4.5, and clue cells on microscopy are the classic Amsel criteria findings for bacterial vaginosis. Candidiasis typically causes thick white discharge, vulvar itching, and a normal or low pH, not clue cells. Trichomoniasis often causes a frothy yellow-green discharge and motile trichomonads, not clue cells specifically. Atrophic vaginitis occurs in low-estrogen states and does not produce a fishy odor or clue cells.
  248. The correct answer is D (Oral metronidazole given systemically to both partners): trichomoniasis is a sexually transmitted infection that requires systemic oral metronidazole because it can infect deeper genitourinary tissue, and partners must be treated simultaneously to prevent reinfection. Topical clotrimazole and oral fluconazole treat candidiasis, not trichomoniasis. Topical metronidazole gel does not achieve adequate systemic levels to treat trichomoniasis effectively. Doxycycline treats chlamydia, not trichomonas, and failing to treat the partner would allow reinfection.
  249. The correct answer is B (Leukocytosis with a neutrophil predominant left shift): an elevated white count with increased immature neutrophils supports an acute inflammatory process like appendicitis, though it is not definitive on its own. A normal white count does not reliably exclude appendicitis and imaging should still be pursued if clinical suspicion is high. Isolated thrombocytosis is nonspecific and not a hallmark of acute appendicitis. Significant anemia would suggest a different chronic process rather than an acute twelve-hour presentation.
  250. The correct answer is D (Complicated UTI requiring a longer course and catheter evaluation): a urinary catheter and male anatomy with structural risk factors make this a complicated UTI, requiring a longer antibiotic duration and assessment of whether the catheter should be changed or removed. Three-day therapy is designed for uncomplicated cystitis in healthy women, not this scenario. Fever with symptoms is not simple asymptomatic colonization and does warrant treatment. Treating identically to a healthy young woman ignores his underlying risk factors that change management.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Migraine headache without any concerning secondary features): unilateral throbbing pain with photophobia, phonophobia, nausea, and worsening with activity is the classic migraine pattern, and a normal interictal exam supports a primary headache disorder. Tension headaches are typically bilateral, pressing, and not usually associated with nausea or photophobia together. Cluster headaches are shorter, more severe, and accompanied by ipsilateral autonomic symptoms like tearing or nasal congestion. Subarachnoid hemorrhage presents as a single sudden thunderclap headache, not a recurrent pattern over time.
  2. The correct answer is C (Lumbar puncture to assess for xanthochromia and red cells): when CT is negative but clinical suspicion for subarachnoid hemorrhage remains high, especially with a thunderclap onset, lumbar puncture looking for xanthochromia and persistent red cells is the appropriate next step. Discharging with reassurance risks missing a life-threatening bleed. Simply repeating CT without LP delays diagnosis and CT sensitivity decreases over time anyway. A triptan would be inappropriate before a serious cause is excluded, and cervical spine MRI does not evaluate for intracranial bleeding.
  3. The correct answer is B (Giant cell arteritis requiring urgent corticosteroid treatment): a new temporal headache with jaw claudication, scalp tenderness, and markedly elevated inflammatory markers in an older adult is classic for giant cell arteritis, which requires immediate high-dose corticosteroids to prevent vision loss. Tension headache would not elevate inflammatory markers or cause jaw claudication. Trigeminal neuralgia causes brief electric shock-like facial pain rather than a constant headache with elevated ESR. Cluster headache does not elevate inflammatory markers and presents with ipsilateral autonomic features instead.
  4. The correct answer is A (Benign paroxysmal positional vertigo confirmed by Dix-Hallpike maneuver): brief, position-triggered episodes of vertigo without hearing changes are classic for BPPV, caused by displaced otoconia, and the Dix-Hallpike maneuver reproduces the nystagmus to confirm it. Vestibular neuritis causes continuous vertigo lasting days, not brief positional episodes. Stroke would more likely present with additional neurologic signs and is not confirmed by a positional trigger alone. Meniere disease includes fluctuating hearing loss and tinnitus, which are absent here.
  5. The correct answer is D (Presence of dysarthria and limb ataxia alongside vertigo): additional brainstem or cerebellar signs like dysarthria and ataxia strongly point to a central cause such as stroke, and this combination warrants urgent neuroimaging. Positional worsening is more typical of peripheral causes like BPPV. Nausea and vomiting can occur with both central and peripheral vertigo and do not localize the lesion. A spinning sensation alone is a nonspecific symptom common to both central and peripheral vertigo.
  6. The correct answer is E (The combination suggests possible central stroke requiring urgent imaging): in the HINTS exam, a normal head impulse test paired with skew deviation, despite unidirectional nystagmus, is actually more concerning for a central cause like posterior circulation stroke rather than reassuring. An abnormal, not normal, head impulse test is what supports a benign peripheral cause. A normal head impulse test with other central features does not exclude stroke. Skew deviation is a red flag for brainstem pathology, not a benign finding, so urgent imaging is warranted.
  7. The correct answer is C (Bell palsy from peripheral facial nerve dysfunction): involvement of the forehead along with the lower face indicates a peripheral seventh nerve lesion, which is the hallmark of Bell palsy, distinguishing it from central causes. A cortical stroke typically spares the forehead because it receives bilateral innervation, so forehead weakness argues against stroke. Multiple sclerosis can rarely cause facial weakness but is far less common than Bell palsy and usually has other findings. Trigeminal neuralgia causes sensory pain, not motor facial weakness.
  8. The correct answer is B (Oral corticosteroids started within 72 hours of onset): corticosteroids given early in the course of Bell palsy improve the likelihood of complete facial nerve recovery and are the standard of care. Antivirals alone, without steroids, have not shown clear benefit and are not recommended as monotherapy. Surgical decompression is reserved for rare, severe, or refractory cases and is not first-line treatment. Physical therapy may support recovery but does not replace the proven benefit of early corticosteroid treatment.
  9. The correct answer is A (Primary syphilis presenting as a painless chancre): a solitary painless indurated genital ulcer with nontender regional lymphadenopathy is the classic presentation of primary syphilis at the site of inoculation. Secondary syphilis occurs weeks later with a diffuse rash, often involving the palms and soles, not an isolated ulcer. Latent syphilis is asymptomatic by definition, which does not match this active ulcer. Tertiary syphilis occurs years later with gummas or cardiovascular and neurologic involvement, not an early ulcer.
  10. The correct answer is D (Urgent evaluation including brain and vascular imaging): a TIA carries a high short-term risk of subsequent stroke, so urgent brain imaging, carotid and vascular assessment, and cardiac evaluation are needed promptly, often with same-day workup. Reassurance with delayed follow-up ignores the significant early stroke risk after a TIA. Aspirin alone without further evaluation misses identifying a treatable underlying cause like carotid stenosis or atrial fibrillation. Complete symptom resolution does not lower the urgency, since TIA is itself a warning sign requiring immediate assessment.
  11. The correct answer is C (Neuroimaging with brain MRI along with basic metabolic studies): a first unprovoked seizure in an adult warrants brain MRI to look for a structural cause plus metabolic and glucose testing to exclude reversible provoking factors. Genetic testing is not first-line and is reserved for specific suspected syndromes. Lumbar puncture is only indicated if there is fever, meningeal signs, or other suspicion of infection, not routinely. Starting long-term antiepileptic therapy without first determining the underlying cause is premature after a single unprovoked event.
  12. The correct answer is A (Electroencephalogram to look for epileptiform abnormalities): EEG helps identify epileptiform discharges that increase the estimated risk of seizure recurrence and informs shared decision-making about starting antiepileptic therapy. Repeating the MRI the next day adds no new information since it was already normal. Lumbar puncture is not indicated without signs of infection or elevated intracranial pressure. A chest x-ray addresses aspiration risk, not seizure recurrence risk, and is not part of the standard first-seizure workup.
  13. The correct answer is B (Acute pancreatitis related to alcohol use): epigastric pain radiating to the back with markedly elevated lipase and a history of heavy alcohol use is classic for acute pancreatitis, one of the two most common causes alongside gallstones. Cholecystitis causes right upper quadrant pain with Murphy sign, not epigastric pain radiating straight to the back. A perforated ulcer causes sudden diffuse peritonitis with free air on imaging, not isolated elevated lipase. Mesenteric ischemia typically presents with pain out of proportion to exam in a patient with vascular risk factors, not this lipase pattern.
  14. The correct answer is E (Small bowel obstruction likely from adhesions): a prior abdominal surgery, distension, obstipation, and dilated small bowel loops with air-fluid levels on x-ray are classic for adhesive small bowel obstruction, the most common cause in patients with prior surgery. Gastroenteritis does not typically cause obstipation or dilated obstructive loops on imaging. Irritable bowel syndrome is a functional diagnosis without these objective obstructive findings. Diverticulitis would show localized sigmoid inflammation, not diffuse small bowel dilation.
  15. The correct answer is D (Hypokalemia with an elevated BUN reflecting dehydration): significant vomiting and diarrhea commonly cause potassium loss and volume depletion, reflected here by low potassium and an elevated BUN out of proportion to creatinine, both requiring prompt fluid and electrolyte repletion. Hyperglycemia is not a described finding and is not the primary concern in this scenario. Elevated sodium is not present in this panel and free water restriction would not be indicated for dehydration. Elevated calcium is not a typical finding in gastroenteritis and is not shown here.
  16. The correct answer is C (Intramuscular ceftriaxone, with doxycycline added if chlamydia is not excluded): ceftriaxone is the recommended first-line treatment for gonorrhea, and because coinfection with chlamydia is common, empiric doxycycline is added unless chlamydia has already been excluded. Azithromycin alone is no longer recommended as monotherapy for gonorrhea due to rising resistance concerns. Waiting for chlamydia results delays necessary treatment for the confirmed gonorrhea infection. Ciprofloxacin is not used for gonorrhea due to widespread fluoroquinolone resistance.
  17. The correct answer is A (Topical or oral azole antifungal therapy): thick white discharge, itching, normal to low pH, and pseudohyphae on KOH prep confirm vulvovaginal candidiasis, which responds to azole antifungals such as fluconazole or topical clotrimazole. Metronidazole treats bacterial vaginosis and trichomoniasis, not fungal infection. Ceftriaxone treats gonorrhea and would have no effect on a fungal infection. Doxycycline treats chlamydia and has no antifungal activity for this presentation.
  18. The correct answer is B (Tension type headache without associated migrainous features): bilateral, pressing, band-like pain without nausea, photophobia, phonophobia, or worsening with activity is the classic description of tension type headache, the most common primary headache disorder. Migraine typically involves unilateral throbbing pain plus nausea or photophobia, which are absent here. Cluster headache is unilateral, severe, and accompanied by autonomic features, none of which are described. Medication overuse headache requires a history of frequent analgesic use, which is not mentioned in this stem.
  19. The correct answer is D (Meniere disease from endolymphatic hydrops): the combination of episodic vertigo, fluctuating low-frequency hearing loss, tinnitus, and aural fullness is the classic tetrad of Meniere disease, thought to result from excess endolymph fluid pressure. BPPV is triggered by position change and does not cause hearing loss or aural fullness. Vestibular neuritis causes continuous vertigo without fluctuating hearing changes. Acoustic neuroma typically causes progressive, not fluctuating, unilateral hearing loss and tinnitus over time.
  20. The correct answer is C (The gravid uterus displaces the appendix upward as pregnancy progresses): as the uterus enlarges, it pushes the appendix superiorly and laterally, so pain in later pregnancy often localizes higher than the classic McBurney point, making diagnosis more challenging. The appendix does not move to the left side in pregnancy. Pain does not universally present in the epigastrium during pregnancy. Appendicitis remains one of the most common surgical emergencies in pregnancy and must still be considered.
  21. The correct answer is A (Inability to raise the eyebrow and wrinkle the forehead on the affected side): because the forehead receives bilateral cortical innervation, forehead involvement points to a peripheral lesion affecting the entire facial nerve rather than a central lesion. Sparing of the forehead with only lower facial weakness is actually more typical of a central, upper motor neuron lesion like stroke. Preserved eyebrow elevation with lower droop again suggests a central cause, not peripheral Bell palsy. Symmetric strength would mean no facial nerve lesion is present at all.
  22. The correct answer is E (Transient ischemic attack without evidence of infarction): a transient focal neurologic deficit with complete resolution and no infarct on diffusion-weighted MRI meets the tissue-based definition of a TIA, distinguishing it from an actual stroke. A completed stroke implies persistent deficit or evidence of infarction on imaging, neither of which is present. Todd paralysis follows a seizure, which is not described here. Conversion disorder and hemiplegic migraine are diagnoses of exclusion and are far less likely given the vascular risk profile and classic presentation.
  23. The correct answer is B (Uncomplicated cystitis treated with a short oral antibiotic course): dysuria, frequency, and suprapubic discomfort without fever or flank pain, with a positive urinalysis, is classic uncomplicated cystitis, appropriately treated with a short course of an oral antibiotic like nitrofurantoin. Pyelonephritis requires flank pain and systemic symptoms like fever, which are absent here. Vaginitis would show discharge and different wet mount findings, not a positive nitrite urinalysis. Interstitial cystitis is a chronic condition and would not show positive nitrites indicating active bacterial infection.
  24. The correct answer is D (State law typically requires a seizure-free period before driving resumes): most states require a defined seizure-free interval, often three to twelve months depending on the jurisdiction, before driving privileges may resume after a seizure, and physicians should counsel patients accordingly and follow local reporting requirements. Resuming immediately would violate safety standards and most state regulations. A permanent lifetime prohibition after a single seizure is not standard practice in most jurisdictions. A universal five-year wait is not an accurate reflection of typical state requirements.
  25. The correct answer is C (Tubal scarring causing infertility and chronic pelvic pain): PID can cause fallopian tube scarring and adhesions, leading to chronic pelvic pain, infertility, and increased ectopic pregnancy risk, which fits this patient's delayed presentation. Bacterial vaginosis is a distinct condition and is not a direct complication of prior PID treatment.
  26. The correct answer is A (Left lower quadrant pain more commonly suggests diverticulitis while right lower quadrant pain more commonly suggests appendicitis): anatomic location remains a useful clinical clue, since diverticulitis classically involves the sigmoid colon on the left while appendicitis classically presents in the right lower quadrant with periumbilical migration. Appendicitis is not equally likely regardless of side given typical anatomic location of the appendix. Diverticulitis is strongly associated with left lower quadrant disease, so this option is factually incorrect. Right lower quadrant pain in men obviously cannot represent gynecologic pathology, making that option nonsensical.
  27. The correct answer is B (Increase fluid intake and maintain normal dietary calcium): adequate fluid intake to increase urine volume is the most evidence-based intervention, and normal, not restricted, dietary calcium actually helps bind oxalate in the gut and reduce stone risk. Severely restricting calcium can paradoxically increase oxalate absorption and stone formation. Eliminating fruits and vegetables removes helpful citrate sources and is not recommended. High-dose vitamin C supplementation can increase oxalate production and should be avoided in stone formers.
  28. The correct answer is D (Offer expedited partner therapy where legally permitted): expedited partner therapy allows the clinician to provide medication or a prescription for the partner without a direct clinic visit, reducing reinfection risk when partners face barriers to care and where state law allows it. Waiting for symptoms ignores that chlamydia is often asymptomatic and the partner could still transmit infection. Delaying the patient's own treatment is inappropriate and increases her complication risk. Ignoring partner management entirely leads to a cycle of reinfection.
  29. The correct answer is C (The first is bacterial vaginosis and the second is trichomoniasis, which does require partner treatment): clue cells with a positive whiff test define bacterial vaginosis, while motile organisms and strawberry cervix define trichomoniasis, a sexually transmitted infection requiring partner treatment, unlike bacterial vaginosis. The regimens differ somewhat and partner treatment needs are not identical between the two conditions. Bacterial vaginosis does not require partner treatment since it reflects flora imbalance rather than a classic sexually transmitted infection. The two conditions have different causative organisms, not the same one.
  30. The correct answer is E (Urgently evaluate for a secondary cause such as subarachnoid hemorrhage): a sudden, severe headache that is different in character from a patient's usual migraine and reaches peak intensity within a minute is a thunderclap headache and requires urgent evaluation regardless of prior migraine history. Treating it as a routine migraine risks missing a life-threatening cause. A prior history of migraine does not make new thunderclap features safe to ignore. Delaying evaluation to a routine outpatient visit is inappropriate given the acuity and change in pattern described.
  31. The correct answer is B (Left middle cerebral artery territory affecting the motor cortex): right-sided weakness with pronator drift reflects a lesion in the left hemisphere motor pathways, most commonly supplied by the middle cerebral artery, the vessel most frequently involved in ischemic stroke and TIA. Isolated cerebellar involvement typically causes ataxia and coordination problems rather than pure motor drift. Spinal cord lesions would more often produce bilateral or level-specific findings, not this pattern. The ophthalmic artery supplies the retina and would cause visual loss, not limb weakness.
  32. The correct answer is A (Nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs given intravenously or orally): NSAIDs are generally preferred first-line for renal colic because they reduce ureteral smooth muscle spasm and inflammation effectively, often with fewer side effects than opioids for this specific pain type. Benzodiazepines are not indicated for renal colic and do not address the underlying inflammatory pain mechanism.
  33. The correct answer is D (Bacterial meningitis requiring emergent evaluation and treatment): fever, headache, neck stiffness, photophobia, and a petechial rash together are highly concerning for bacterial meningitis, particularly meningococcemia, requiring emergent lumbar puncture, blood cultures, and prompt empiric antibiotics. Tension headache does not cause fever, neck stiffness, or a petechial rash. Migraine with aura does not present with fever or a rash. Cluster headache lacks fever and meningeal signs and is not associated with a petechial rash.
  34. Non-contrast CT alone has limited sensitivity for small posterior fossa infarcts, contrary to option A's claim. Cervical spine x-ray does not evaluate brain tissue for stroke.   35. Complete sparing of the lower face would not represent facial weakness at all. Symmetric strength in all regions means there is no facial weakness present.
  36. The correct answer is A (Todd paralysis following a focal seizure): transient focal weakness after witnessed rhythmic jerking movements is characteristic of Todd paralysis, a temporary postictal phenomenon that typically resolves within twenty-four hours. A TIA would not typically be preceded by rhythmic jerking movements, which suggest seizure activity instead. Ischemic stroke does not typically resolve spontaneously within an hour, especially following a seizure-like event. Hypoglycemia can cause neurologic symptoms but would not specifically explain preceding rhythmic jerking movements.
  37. The correct answer is D (Sudden severe unilateral pain with an enlarged ovary on ultrasound): ovarian torsion classically presents with sudden, severe, unilateral pain often associated with nausea and an enlarged ovary with abnormal or absent Doppler flow on ultrasound. Gradual onset over days is more typical of appendicitis or another subacute process, not torsion. Periumbilical to right lower quadrant migration and a positive psoas sign are classic appendicitis findings, not torsion. A positive Murphy sign points to cholecystitis, an unrelated right upper quadrant process.
  38. The correct answer is C (Offer HIV testing as part of the current STI evaluation): guidelines recommend offering HIV testing whenever a patient is being evaluated for another sexually transmitted infection, since shared risk factors make coinfection more likely and early detection improves outcomes. Testing should not be limited only to those reporting injection drug use, since sexual transmission is a major route. Waiting for symptoms misses the opportunity for early diagnosis, since HIV is often asymptomatic for years. Testing should be routinely offered, not only performed when a patient specifically requests it.
  39. The correct answer is E (Idiopathic intracranial hypertension requiring further evaluation): a headache worse with lying down or coughing, transient visual obscurations, papilledema, and obesity in a woman of reproductive age are classic for idiopathic intracranial hypertension, requiring neuroimaging and often lumbar puncture with opening pressure measurement. Tension headache does not cause papilledema or visual graying episodes. Migraine without red flags would not include papilledema, an objective sign of elevated intracranial pressure. Simple dehydration does not cause papilledema and would not explain this constellation of findings.
  40. The correct answer is B (Automatisms such as lip smacking with altered awareness): temporal lobe seizures classically produce automatisms like lip smacking or fumbling along with impaired awareness, reflecting the temporal lobe's role in memory and behavior circuits. Visual flashing lights are more typical of occipital lobe seizures, not temporal. Sudden bilateral limb jerking without warning suggests a generalized rather than focal temporal onset. Numbness spreading up one leg is more consistent with a parietal lobe sensory seizure, not temporal lobe involvement.
  41.   42. Plain x-ray has poor sensitivity for appendicitis and is not a recommended diagnostic tool for this condition.   43.   44.   45. The correct answer is B (Lipase greater than three times the upper limit of normal): lipase elevated more than three times the upper reference limit is the most sensitive and specific lab criterion for acute pancreatitis. Amylase elevation is less specific and can be elevated in many other conditions. Mild AST elevation only suggests a possible biliary component, not pancreatitis itself. Hypocalcemia can occur in severe pancreatitis from saponification but is not diagnostic on its own.
  46. Tubo-ovarian abscess and appendicitis would present with more prolonged pain, fever, or focal tenderness rather than a brief cyclic pattern.
  47. The correct answer is D (Chronic mesenteric ischemia): postprandial pain causing food fear and weight loss in a patient with widespread atherosclerosis is the classic presentation of intestinal angina from chronic mesenteric ischemia. Gastric outlet obstruction typically causes early satiety and vomiting rather than pain tied specifically to digestion timing. Celiac disease presents with chronic diarrhea and malabsorption rather than exertional-type postprandial pain. Irritable bowel syndrome does not typically cause significant unintentional weight loss.
  48. The correct answer is B (Giardia lamblia): prolonged greasy, foul-smelling diarrhea with bloating and flatulence after freshwater exposure during camping is classic for giardiasis, which causes malabsorptive rather than inflammatory diarrhea. Salmonella and Shigella typically cause acute inflammatory diarrhea with fever and sometimes blood. Norovirus causes a shorter self-limited illness with vomiting predominating over the two-week greasy diarrhea pattern described here.
  49. The correct answer is A (Hemolytic uremic syndrome): bloody diarrhea from Shiga toxin-producing E. coli followed by pallor, oliguria, and confusion strongly suggests hemolytic uremic syndrome, a triad of hemolytic anemia, thrombocytopenia, and acute kidney injury. Antibiotics should be avoided since they can increase toxin release and this risk. Reactive arthritis and Guillain-Barre are post-infectious complications that develop later and present differently. Toxic megacolon and intussusception do not present with this systemic hemolytic and renal picture.
  50. The correct answer is A (Oral rehydration and supportive care): most cases of traveler's diarrhea are self-limited and mild illness in an otherwise healthy patient is managed supportively with oral rehydration. Empiric antibiotics are reserved for moderate to severe symptoms or high fever. Stool culture is not required before treating mild self-limited illness. Intravenous fluids are unnecessary unless the patient shows signs of significant dehydration.
  51. Isolated hypernatremia or hypokalemia alone would not fully explain the severity of his lethargic presentation. There is no artifact here since all values are consistent with true volume loss.
  52. Celiac disease and inflammatory bowel disease are chronic conditions that would not explain an acute simultaneous cluster of illness.   53.   54.   55. The correct answer is C (Triple therapy with a proton pump inhibitor, clarithromycin, and amoxicillin): standard first-line therapy for Helicobacter pylori in areas of low clarithromycin resistance combines a proton pump inhibitor with clarithromycin and amoxicillin for ten to fourteen days. Amoxicillin alone or proton pump inhibitor monotherapy will not adequately eradicate the organism. Bismuth alone is part of quadruple therapy but is insufficient by itself for eradication.
  56. The correct answer is C (Psoas sign): pain elicited by extending the hip, which stretches the iliopsoas muscle near an inflamed retrocecal appendix, defines the psoas sign. Rovsing sign is pain in the right lower quadrant elicited by palpating the left lower quadrant. Obturator sign involves pain with internal rotation of a flexed hip. Murphy sign refers to inspiratory arrest during right upper quadrant palpation and is associated with cholecystitis, not appendicitis.

Mesenteric adenitis does not typically progress this way, and antiemetics would not cause peritoneal signs to appear.

Intravenous antibiotics and admission are reserved for pyelonephritis or systemic illness. Imaging is not routinely needed for a first uncomplicated cystitis episode.   61. Nitrites or leukocyte esterase alone simply indicate a urinary tract infection without localizing to the kidney. Elevated WBC count without correlating urine findings would be nonspecific.
  62. The correct answer is D (No antibiotic treatment since he is asymptomatic): asymptomatic bacteriuria in a catheterized patient without fever, pain, or systemic symptoms does not require antibiotics, since treatment does not improve outcomes and promotes resistance. Broad-spectrum antibiotics or admission would be inappropriate overtreatment for an asymptomatic finding. Prophylactic antibiotics with catheter change are not indicated for asymptomatic bacteriuria.   63. The correct answer is B (Treat with an appropriate oral antibiotic despite lack of symptoms): asymptomatic bacteriuria in pregnancy is treated to prevent progression to pyelonephritis and associated risks of preterm labor, unlike in nonpregnant adults where it is left untreated. Delaying treatment with a repeat culture risks progression to symptomatic infection. Withholding treatment entirely is inappropriate specifically in pregnancy. Urology referral is unnecessary for a straightforward positive culture in pregnancy.
  64. Simple pyelonephritis does not produce parenchymal gas. Renal abscess would show a discrete fluid collection rather than gas throughout the parenchyma. Papillary necrosis presents with sloughed tissue and hematuria rather than gas formation.
  65.   66. The correct answer is D (Conservative management with hydration, analgesia, and observation for spontaneous passage): stones under 5mm have a high likelihood of spontaneous passage, so conservative management with hydration, pain control, and possibly a medical expulsive agent is appropriate when there is no infection or significant obstruction. Immediate lithotripsy or ureteroscopy is reserved for larger stones or failed conservative therapy. Emergency stenting is indicated for infected or obstructing stones, not this stable presentation.
  67. The correct answer is E (Urgent decompression with a ureteral stent or nephrostomy plus intravenous antibiotics): an obstructing stone with signs of systemic infection is a urologic emergency requiring urgent decompression combined with intravenous antibiotics to prevent urosepsis. Oral antibiotics alone are inadequate for this level of illness. Watchful waiting or increasing fluids ignores the infected obstruction and risks rapid deterioration. Lithotripsy is contraindicated in the setting of active infection since it can worsen sepsis.
  68. The correct answer is D (Malignancy-associated hypercalcemia or another PTH-independent cause): hypercalcemia with a suppressed parathyroid hormone level points away from a parathyroid source and toward a PTH-independent cause such as malignancy or excess vitamin D. Primary hyperparathyroidism would show an elevated or inappropriately normal parathyroid hormone level, not a low one. Distal renal tubular acidosis causes stones through a different mechanism involving urinary alkalinization, not hypercalcemia. Excess vitamin C intake is a minor contributor to oxalate stones but does not explain hypercalcemia with low PTH.
  69. The correct answer is C (Neisseria gonorrhoeae): a short incubation period with copious purulent discharge is characteristic of gonococcal urethritis, in contrast to the more scant, mucoid discharge and longer incubation typical of chlamydia.   70. The correct answer is C (Treat the partner presumptively without requiring testing first): expedited partner therapy is recommended for chlamydia, treating sexual partners presumptively without requiring a prior visit or positive test to reduce reinfection and further transmission. Withholding treatment until symptoms develop or testing confirms infection increases the risk of continued transmission and reinfection of the index patient. Testing before treatment delays care and is not required under current guidance for this infection.
  71. The correct answer is B (Primary syphilis): a solitary, painless, indurated ulcer with a clean base and nontender regional lymphadenopathy describes the classic chancre of primary syphilis. Herpes causes multiple painful vesicular or ulcerative lesions, not a single painless ulcer. Chancroid produces a painful ulcer with tender lymphadenopathy, the opposite pattern described here. Lymphogranuloma venereum and granuloma inguinale have different ulcer and lymph node characteristics that do not match this classic presentation.
  72. A negative whiff test simply argues against bacterial vaginosis and does not address pelvic inflammatory disease risk.   73. The correct answer is A (Hospital admission for intravenous antibiotics and possible drainage): a large tubo-ovarian abscess that fails to improve on outpatient therapy requires inpatient management with intravenous antibiotics and consideration of image-guided or surgical drainage to prevent rupture and sepsis. Continuing or switching oral antibiotics without escalation risks progression to a life-threatening rupture. Observation without treatment change is inappropriate given the lack of clinical improvement and abscess size.
  74. The correct answer is C (Bacterial vaginosis): thin gray homogeneous discharge, a fishy odor accentuated by intercourse, elevated pH, and clue cells on microscopy together fulfill the classic diagnostic criteria for bacterial vaginosis. Candidiasis typically presents with thick white curd-like discharge and vulvar itching without a fishy odor. Trichomoniasis causes frothy yellow-green discharge often with cervical petechiae. Atrophic vaginitis occurs in estrogen-deficient states and does not produce clue cells.
  75. The correct answer is A (Vulvovaginal candidiasis): intense itching with thick curd-like discharge, vulvar erythema, and a normal vaginal pH without clue cells or motile trichomonads is characteristic of candidiasis, which is treated with topical or oral antifungal therapy. Trichomoniasis typically raises vaginal pH and shows motile flagellated organisms on wet mount. Bacterial vaginosis produces clue cells and a fishy odor rather than intense itching. Chlamydial cervicitis usually presents with cervical discharge and possible bleeding rather than vulvar pruritus.
  76. The correct answer is E (Oral metronidazole for the patient and her sexual partner): trichomoniasis, confirmed by motile organisms and the classic strawberry cervix appearance, is treated with oral metronidazole for both the patient and her partner since it is a sexually transmitted infection requiring partner treatment to prevent reinfection. Topical therapies are less effective for trichomoniasis than oral treatment. Fluconazole treats fungal infections, not a protozoal infection like trichomoniasis. This infection does not resolve reliably without treatment.
  77. The correct answer is D (Atrophic vaginitis): postmenopausal vaginal dryness, burning, and painful intercourse with thin pale mucosa and loss of rugae reflect estrogen deficiency causing atrophic vaginitis, treated with vaginal moisturizers or topical estrogen. Bacterial vaginosis, candidiasis, and trichomoniasis are infectious causes that would present with discharge, odor, or itching rather than this atrophic mucosal appearance.
  78. The correct answer is C (Migraine with aura): unilateral throbbing pain preceded by visual disturbances such as zigzag lines, accompanied by nausea and photophobia, is the classic presentation of migraine with aura. Tension-type headache is typically bilateral and pressing without aura or significant nausea. Cluster headache causes severe unilateral periorbital pain with autonomic features and is much shorter in duration. Medication overuse headache develops from frequent analgesic use rather than this episodic aura pattern.
  79. The correct answer is D (Cluster headache): severe unilateral periorbital pain with ipsilateral autonomic features like tearing and nasal congestion occurring in a clustered pattern, often nocturnally, is characteristic of cluster headache. Trigeminal neuralgia causes brief electric shock-like facial pain rather than sustained periorbital pain with autonomic symptoms. Migraine without aura lacks the prominent ipsilateral autonomic features described. Sinusitis typically presents with facial pressure, congestion, and purulent discharge rather than this clustered nocturnal pattern.
  80. The correct answer is C (Giant cell arteritis): new headache with jaw claudication, temporal tenderness, and visual changes in an older adult are classic red flags for giant cell arteritis, which requires urgent high-dose corticosteroids to prevent permanent vision loss while confirmatory testing is arranged. Tension headache, migraine, and cluster headache do not typically cause jaw claudication or threaten vision. Trigeminal neuralgia causes brief facial pain but not this systemic and visual symptom pattern.
  81. The correct answer is D (Subarachnoid hemorrhage): a thunderclap headache described as the worst of the patient's life combined with meningeal signs such as nuchal rigidity is a classic presentation of subarachnoid hemorrhage and warrants emergent CT head and further workup. Migraine and tension headache do not typically produce true meningismus or reach maximal intensity within seconds. Sinusitis causes facial pressure rather than sudden severe head pain with meningeal signs.
  82. The correct answer is D (Idiopathic intracranial hypertension): daily headache with transient visual obscurations, pulsatile tinnitus, and papilledema in an obese woman of reproductive age is classic for idiopathic intracranial hypertension, requiring lumbar puncture to confirm elevated opening pressure and urgent ophthalmologic evaluation. Migraine and tension headache do not cause papilledema. Trigeminal neuralgia causes brief facial pain without papilledema or visual obscurations.
  83. The correct answer is D (Medication overuse headache): a pattern of worsening chronic headache that correlates with frequent analgesic use is characteristic of medication overuse headache, managed by gradually discontinuing the offending medication. New daily persistent headache arises abruptly without this progressive medication-use correlation. Cluster headache follows a distinct episodic pattern with autonomic features not described here. Giant cell arteritis occurs in older adults with additional systemic and visual symptoms.
  84. The correct answer is A (Central vertigo from a posterior circulation stroke): vertical nystagmus that fails to suppress with visual fixation, combined with dysarthria and limb ataxia, points to a central cause such as a cerebellar or brainstem stroke rather than a peripheral vestibular disorder. Benign paroxysmal positional vertigo and vestibular neuritis produce horizontal or torsional nystagmus that suppresses with fixation and lack these focal neurologic findings. Meniere disease and migrainous vertigo also do not typically present with dysarthria and ataxia.
  85. The correct answer is C (Benign paroxysmal positional vertigo): brief vertigo episodes triggered by positional changes such as rolling over, caused by displaced otoconia in the posterior semicircular canal and confirmed with a positive Dix-Hallpike maneuver, define benign paroxysmal positional vertigo. Vestibular neuritis causes continuous vertigo lasting days rather than brief positional episodes. Meniere disease involves prolonged vertigo with hearing loss and tinnitus. Acoustic neuroma causes gradual progressive symptoms rather than sudden brief positional vertigo.
  86. The correct answer is D (Vestibular neuritis): continuous vertigo lasting days after a viral illness, without hearing loss or focal neurologic findings, is typical of vestibular neuritis from inflammation of the vestibular nerve. Meniere disease includes fluctuating hearing loss and tinnitus, which are absent here. Benign paroxysmal positional vertigo causes brief episodic vertigo rather than continuous symptoms. Posterior circulation stroke would be expected to show focal neurologic deficits, which are explicitly absent in this case.
  87. The correct answer is A (Meniere disease): recurrent vertigo episodes lasting minutes to hours with fluctuating hearing loss, tinnitus, and aural fullness are the classic tetrad of Meniere disease, thought to result from endolymphatic hydrops. Benign paroxysmal positional vertigo causes only brief seconds-long episodes without hearing changes. Vestibular neuritis and labyrinthitis cause continuous rather than episodic vertigo, and labyrinthitis includes hearing loss but without the fluctuating recurrent pattern described.
  88. The correct answer is D (Bell palsy): unilateral facial weakness that includes the forehead, indicating a peripheral cranial nerve VII lesion, without limb weakness or sensory deficits, is characteristic of Bell palsy rather than a central process. Ischemic stroke typically spares the forehead due to bilateral cortical innervation of the upper face and often includes limb involvement. Trigeminal neuralgia causes facial pain rather than weakness. Multiple sclerosis can cause facial weakness but usually with other neurologic findings and a different clinical course.
  89. The correct answer is B (Oral corticosteroids started within 72 hours of onset): early oral corticosteroids given within 72 hours of symptom onset improve the likelihood of complete recovery in Bell palsy and are the standard of care. Antiviral monotherapy without steroids has not shown clear benefit alone. While many cases improve without treatment, steroids improve outcomes and should not be withheld when appropriate. Intravenous immunoglobulin and inpatient high-dose steroids are not standard therapy for typical Bell palsy.
  90. The correct answer is D (An upper motor neuron lesion above the facial nucleus, such as a stroke): forehead sparing occurs because the upper face receives bilateral cortical innervation, so a central lesion above the facial nucleus like a stroke spares forehead movement while causing lower facial weakness, arm drift, and slurred speech. A peripheral facial nerve lesion, as in Bell palsy, would involve the entire face including the forehead. Trigeminal nerve lesions affect sensation and mastication, not facial movement. Neuromuscular junction disorders typically cause fluctuating weakness rather than this focal pattern with limb findings.
  91. The correct answer is A (The narrow bony facial canal within the temporal bone): the facial nerve travels through a narrow, rigid bony canal in the temporal bone, so any inflammation or swelling compresses the nerve within this confined space and produces the weakness seen in Bell palsy. The subarachnoid space and internal capsule are not part of the facial nerve's peripheral course. The cavernous sinus is relevant to other cranial nerves, not the facial nerve's canal segment.
  92. The correct answer is C (Urgent evaluation including brain imaging and vascular workup given high stroke risk): a transient ischemic attack carries substantial short-term stroke risk, so patients require urgent evaluation including brain imaging, vascular imaging, and cardiac assessment to identify the underlying cause and initiate secondary prevention. Reassurance or discharge without workup ignores this elevated risk. Starting aspirin without any evaluation would miss identifiable causes needing more specific treatment such as anticoagulation for atrial fibrillation.

  94. The correct answer is B (Internal carotid artery and its downstream middle cerebral artery territory): carotid imaging evaluates for stenosis in the internal carotid artery, a common embolic source that can send clot fragments into the middle cerebral artery territory and cause anterior circulation TIA or stroke symptoms. Vertebral artery imaging would assess posterior circulation instead, which is a distinct territory. Coronary circulation and the portal venous system are unrelated to cerebral embolic stroke risk.
  95.   96. Lumbar puncture is reserved for suspected infection or specific clinical concerns, not performed routinely on every patient.   98. The correct answer is B (Administer benzodiazepines immediately to terminate the seizure): this presentation describes status epilepticus, a medical emergency requiring immediate benzodiazepine administration followed by a second-line antiepileptic agent to terminate seizure activity and prevent neurologic injury. Waiting and observing risks ongoing brain injury from prolonged seizure activity. Oral medications act too slowly for this emergency, and delaying treatment for consultation or outpatient testing is inappropriate given the time-critical nature of status epilepticus.
  99. The correct answer is C (Focal onset seizure with retained awareness): seizure activity beginning in one limb with preserved consciousness that then spreads, sometimes called a Jacksonian march, describes a focal onset seizure with retained awareness. Absence seizures involve brief staring spells without motor jerking. Generalized tonic-clonic seizures involve bilateral convulsions with loss of consciousness from onset. Myoclonic seizures are brief, shock-like jerks rather than a spreading focal pattern with retained awareness.
  100. Progression of underlying epilepsy severity is less likely to explain a seizure timed closely with a new medication. Antibiotics do not cause acute structural brain changes that would explain a breakthrough seizure this quickly.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1.   3.   4.
