They also do not replace other established osteoarthritis management strategies.
  5. The correct answer is B (Osteoporosis is present and pharmacologic treatment such as a bisphosphonate should be initiated): a T-score of -2.5 or lower at any site defines osteoporosis, and these values meet that threshold, warranting pharmacologic treatment such as a bisphosphonate. These results are well below the normal range and cannot be classified as normal bone density. The T-scores are lower than the osteopenia range of -1.0 to -2.5, meeting full osteoporosis criteria instead. The results are clearly diagnostic and require action, not further inconclusive observation.
  6. The correct answer is A (Initiate pharmacologic therapy since FRAX risk exceeds the treatment threshold of 20 percent for major fracture risk): current guidelines recommend pharmacologic treatment in osteopenic patients whose FRAX ten-year major fracture risk exceeds 20 percent, even without a T-score in the osteoporosis range. Withholding treatment based on T-score alone ignores the elevated FRAX-based risk. Repeating DEXA in ten years without action delays needed intervention given her elevated fracture risk. Dietary measures alone are insufficient at this elevated fracture risk level.
  7. The correct answer is C (Continue bisphosphonate therapy and reassess fracture risk and duration at future intervals, considering a drug holiday after about five years): improving T-scores on bisphosphonate therapy support continuing treatment with periodic reassessment, and a bisphosphonate drug holiday is typically considered after about five years in appropriate candidates. Stopping therapy immediately is premature since she remains in the osteoporosis range. A bone biopsy is not indicated to confirm DEXA improvement. Switching drug classes is unwarranted since the current therapy is clearly working.

Recognizing this specific pattern is critical since cerebral edema can be rapidly fatal if missed.
  145. The correct answer is C (Recognize severe metabolic acidosis requiring close monitoring during treatment): A pH of 7.02 with a bicarbonate of 8 reflects severe metabolic acidosis, which warrants close monitoring, often in an intensive care setting, during fluid and insulin therapy. Calling this mild or denying acidosis entirely misreads clearly abnormal values. Severe acidosis is precisely when standard DKA fluid and insulin protocols are most essential, not less needed.
  146. The correct answer is D (Hyperglycemia causes an osmotic shift that dilutes the measured sodium): Severe hyperglycemia pulls water into the vascular space, diluting the measured sodium, so a corrected sodium calculation better reflects true volume and free water status. Sodium correction is a standard and necessary part of DKA assessment, not something to skip. Glucose dilutes rather than falsely elevates sodium, and the correction is unrelated to the potassium value.
  147. The correct answer is E (Fluid deficits tend to be larger, requiring more aggressive volume repletion): Hyperosmolar hyperglycemic state typically involves larger fluid deficits than DKA and requires aggressive, carefully monitored volume repletion alongside insulin. Insulin still plays a role once fluids are underway, so it is not contraindicated. Bicarbonate is not routinely needed since significant acidosis is usually absent, and potassium still requires monitoring since insulin therapy still shifts potassium intracellularly regardless of ketosis severity.
  148. The correct answer is A (Give subcutaneous insulin and overlap with the infusion for one to two hours before stopping it): Transitioning from an insulin infusion to subcutaneous insulin requires overlapping the two for one to two hours since subcutaneous insulin takes time to reach therapeutic levels, preventing rebound hyperglycemia or ketosis. Stopping the infusion abruptly risks this gap in coverage. Oral agents are not appropriate for type 1 diabetes, and discharge should wait until the patient is stable on the new regimen with appropriate follow up arranged.
  149. The correct answer is B (An underlying infection or other acute precipitating illness): Infection is one of the most common precipitants of DKA and should always be actively sought through history, exam, and targeted testing, since untreated infection will prevent resolution of the ketoacidosis. Shoe size, unrelated family history, and insulin pen brand have no bearing on identifying the acute trigger. Missed insulin doses are also common, but infection must still be excluded even when a clear nonadherence trigger is found.
  150. The correct answer is C (Insulin is still needed to fully suppress ketogenesis and close the anion gap): Even after glucose normalizes, ongoing insulin is required to shut off the ketogenic process and fully close the anion gap, since glucose can normalize well before the underlying acidosis resolves. Believing insulin has no further role or that feeling well confirms resolution risks premature discontinuation and rebound ketoacidosis. Anion gap closure, not glucose level alone, is the true marker of DKA resolution.
  151. The correct answer is D (Administer intramuscular epinephrine immediately into the anterolateral thigh): Intramuscular epinephrine into the anterolateral thigh is the first line, immediate treatment for anaphylaxis and should never be delayed for other therapies. Antihistamines and bronchodilators can be adjuncts but do not treat the underlying mast cell mediated cardiovascular and airway compromise. Waiting for progression risks rapid deterioration, and corticosteroids alone act too slowly for acute anaphylaxis.
  152. The correct answer is A (Observe for several hours given the risk of a biphasic reaction): Biphasic anaphylactic reactions can occur hours after apparent resolution, so an observation period of several hours, sometimes longer for severe reactions, is recommended before discharge. Immediate discharge after initial improvement risks missing a delayed recurrence. Discharge instructions should always include an epinephrine autoinjector prescription, not just antihistamines, given the risk of recurrence.
  153. The correct answer is B (Start an intravenous epinephrine infusion for refractory anaphylaxis): Refractory anaphylactic shock despite repeated intramuscular epinephrine doses warrants escalation to a continuous intravenous epinephrine infusion with close hemodynamic monitoring. Continuing only intramuscular dosing or switching to oral antihistamines is inadequate for ongoing shock. Fluids remain important but do not replace epinephrine as the definitive treatment for the underlying vasodilation and capillary leak.
  154. The correct answer is C (Consider intravenous glucagon since it can bypass beta blockade): Patients on beta blockers can have blunted responses to epinephrine, and intravenous glucagon can help by acting through a non beta adrenergic pathway to improve heart rate and contractility. Simply escalating epinephrine indefinitely without other measures may not fully resolve the resistant shock. Stopping treatment or giving more beta blockade would be dangerous, and inhaled epinephrine is not the primary route for systemic anaphylaxis.
  155. The correct answer is D (Recognize impending airway compromise and prepare for possible advanced airway management): Throat tightness, dysphagia, and hoarseness signal impending airway edema in anaphylaxis and require immediate epinephrine along with preparation for advanced airway management if swelling progresses. Reassurance without action or continuing the causative infusion ignores a rapidly evolving airway emergency. Oral antihistamines act too slowly to address impending airway obstruction.
  156. The correct answer is A (Prescribe an epinephrine autoinjector and refer to an allergist): Every patient discharged after anaphylaxis should receive an epinephrine autoinjector prescription with training on its use and a referral to an allergist for further evaluation and allergen identification. Waiting for a second reaction before recommending avoidance is unsafe. No instructions or relying solely on antihistamines leaves the patient unprepared for a future life threatening exposure.
  157. The correct answer is E (Inject into the anterolateral thigh through clothing if needed, holding briefly): Autoinjectors are designed for intramuscular injection into the anterolateral thigh, which can be done through thin clothing without needing to undress, and the injection should be held briefly per device instructions for full delivery. The upper arm and abdomen are not the standard recommended sites for autoinjector use. Intravenous administration requires diluted formulations and trained personnel, not a bystander using an autoinjector.
  158. The correct answer is B (A drop in systolic blood pressure with associated dizziness): Hypotension with dizziness reflects cardiovascular involvement in anaphylaxis and requires immediate epinephrine and further management, distinguishing a systemic reaction from a purely local one. Localized redness, brief itching, or mild temperature elevation alone do not indicate systemic anaphylaxis and would be managed differently. Recognizing true systemic involvement prevents both under and over treatment.
  159. The correct answer is C (Avoid exercising alone and always carry an epinephrine autoinjector): Patients with exercise induced anaphylaxis should avoid exercising alone, always carry epinephrine, and in food dependent cases avoid eating for several hours before exertion, since the risk of a severe reaction persists. Believing exercise is fully safe with only food timing changes oversimplifies the risk. No precautions or a narrow cold weather focus both ignore the broader recurrent risk pattern.
  160. The correct answer is D (They may help reduce the risk or severity of a biphasic reaction): Corticosteroids are theorized to reduce the risk or severity of a delayed biphasic reaction, even though their slow onset means they do not help the acute presentation. They are not fast acting and cannot substitute for epinephrine in acute treatment. Their role is broader than just treating hives, relating to the overall inflammatory response.
  161. The correct answer is A (Begin aggressive intravenous fluid resuscitation and type and crossmatch blood): A hemodynamically unstable GI bleed requires immediate resuscitation with intravenous fluids and blood products, along with urgent rather than outpatient endoscopy. Oral medications or outpatient follow up are wholly inadequate for active hemorrhagic shock. Waiting for lab results before starting resuscitation in an obviously unstable patient delays life saving treatment.
  162. The correct answer is B (Transfuse packed red blood cells given the severe anemia and active bleeding): A hemoglobin of 6.8 with evidence of active bleeding meets clear criteria for red blood cell transfusion, generally targeting a threshold around 7 in most patients. Being conscious does not rule out significant blood loss requiring transfusion. Oral iron takes weeks to work and has no role in acute severe anemia, and transfusion should not be delayed for endoscopy in a symptomatic, severely anemic patient.
  163. The correct answer is C (Start octreotide and give antibiotic prophylaxis before endoscopy): Suspected variceal bleeding in cirrhosis warrants starting octreotide to reduce splanchnic blood flow and prophylactic antibiotics, both proven to reduce mortality, while urgent endoscopy is arranged. Withholding antibiotics ignores the proven benefit even without confirmed infection, since cirrhotic patients with GI bleeding are at high risk for bacterial translocation. Octreotide has a clear role and should not be delayed until after the diagnosis is endoscopically confirmed.
  164. The correct answer is D (Consult cardiology and gastroenterology together before stopping dual antiplatelet therapy): Balancing life threatening bleeding against stent thrombosis risk requires a coordinated discussion between cardiology and gastroenterology, since abrupt discontinuation of dual antiplatelet therapy, especially soon after stenting, carries serious thrombosis risk. Unilateral decisions to stop or continue both agents without specialist input risk either uncontrolled bleeding or stent occlusion. This kind of high stakes tradeoff should not be made by one clinician in isolation.
  165. The correct answer is A (Recognize this pattern as supportive of an upper GI bleeding source): A disproportionately elevated BUN to creatinine ratio in GI bleeding reflects increased urea absorption from digested blood in the small intestine, supporting an upper source. This pattern is classically associated with upper, not lower, GI bleeding. While chronic kidney disease affects baseline creatinine, the disproportionate ratio elevation specifically points toward blood digestion as the driving mechanism here.
  166. The correct answer is B (Arrange for urgent colonoscopy or CT angiography depending on stability): Ongoing hematochezia with hemodynamic instability warrants urgent evaluation, with colonoscopy after bowel preparation if feasible or CT angiography if the patient cannot be stabilized enough for prep, to identify and potentially treat the bleeding source. Outpatient management or assuming spontaneous resolution is inappropriate for an unstable bleed. Laxatives would not be given emergently and could worsen instability.
  167. The correct answer is E (Start a high dose intravenous proton pump inhibitor): High dose intravenous proton pump inhibitor therapy started before endoscopy can reduce the severity of endoscopic findings and rebleeding risk in suspected peptic ulcer bleeding. Histamine-2 blockers and antacids are less effective for this purpose. Withholding acid suppression or choosing oral over intravenous dosing in a patient with active upper GI bleeding delays optimal acid control.
  168. The correct answer is C (Monitor hemoglobin trends and observe for rebleeding after the procedure): Even after successful endoscopic hemostasis, patients need continued monitoring of hemoglobin and clinical status for rebleeding before discharge. Diverticular bleeding still carries some rebleeding risk despite clipping, so assuming it is eliminated is incorrect. Starting anticoagulation right after treating an active bleed would be premature and increase rebleeding risk.
  169. The correct answer is D (Hold warfarin and reverse it with an appropriate reversal agent given active bleeding): Active major GI bleeding on warfarin with a supratherapeutic INR warrants holding the medication and using an appropriate reversal strategy, such as prothrombin complex concentrate and vitamin K, to control bleeding. Continuing or increasing warfarin in this setting would worsen hemorrhage. Delaying reversal for days in the setting of active bleeding risks ongoing blood loss and instability.
  170. The correct answer is A (Use a restrictive transfusion threshold, generally transfusing below 7 g/dL): In hemodynamically stable patients with GI bleeding, especially those with cirrhosis, a restrictive transfusion strategy with a threshold around 7 g/dL has been shown to improve outcomes compared to more liberal transfusion. Transfusing at a stable 8.5 without further indication or targeting a higher threshold risks complications like increased portal pressure and rebleeding. Refusing to ever transfuse regardless of severity ignores clear indications that can still arise.
  171. The correct answer is B (Give empiric naloxone, thiamine, and dextrose as indicated by the coma cocktail approach): In undifferentiated altered mental status, empiric treatment with naloxone for possible opioid overdose, thiamine before dextrose to prevent precipitating Wernicke encephalopathy, and dextrose for hypoglycemia should not be delayed while broader workup proceeds. Waiting for a full toxicology screen wastes critical time. Fluids alone do not address reversible causes, and assuming a psychiatric cause without ruling out medical causes first is unsafe.
  172. The correct answer is C (Recognize severe hyponatremia as a likely cause of her altered mental status): A sodium of 118 is severely low and a well recognized cause of confusion, lethargy, and even seizures, requiring careful, controlled correction to avoid osmotic demyelination. Calling this normal or unrelated ignores a clearly abnormal and clinically significant value. Attributing symptoms to age alone without addressing a severe, reversible metabolic abnormality could miss a treatable cause.
  173. The correct answer is D (Rapid correction risks osmotic demyelination syndrome): Correcting chronic severe hyponatremia too quickly can cause osmotic demyelination syndrome, a serious and often irreversible neurologic injury, so correction rates are carefully limited per established guidelines. Claiming no risk exists or that gradual correction is only about comfort understates a real danger. Even severe symptoms do not justify uncontrolled rapid correction, though acute severe symptomatic hyponatremia may warrant a small emergent bolus under close monitoring.
  174. The correct answer is A (Give intravenous dextrose and monitor closely given prolonged sulfonylurea effect): Sulfonylurea induced hypoglycemia can be prolonged and recurrent, so after initial intravenous dextrose, extended monitoring and repeat glucose checks are needed, sometimes with admission. Discharging after a single dose risks recurrent dangerous hypoglycemia. Starting insulin during hypoglycemia would be actively harmful, and attributing symptoms to dementia without confirming glucose normalization misses the reversible cause entirely.
  175. The correct answer is B (Give thiamine immediately to treat suspected Wernicke encephalopathy): The classic triad of confusion, ataxia, and ophthalmoplegia strongly suggests Wernicke encephalopathy, a clinical diagnosis requiring immediate thiamine before or with any glucose to prevent worsening or precipitating the condition. Giving dextrose without thiamine first can worsen the encephalopathy. Attributing this specific triad to simple intoxication or delaying for imaging risks permanent neurologic damage from a treatable emergency.
  176. The correct answer is C (Recognize infection as a common precipitant of delirium in older adults): Infections, including urinary tract infections, are among the most common precipitants of delirium in older adults, and treating the underlying infection often improves mental status. Believing infection cannot cause altered mental status is incorrect and could lead to missed treatable causes. Assuming baseline dementia or normal aging explains an acute change ignores the importance of identifying and treating reversible precipitants.
  177. The correct answer is D (Allow a period of postictal observation while checking glucose and vital signs): A brief self terminating seizure with a normal postictal course generally needs observation, glucose and vital sign checks, and reassessment as consciousness returns, rather than aggressive intervention. Immediate antiepileptic loading or intubation is unnecessary for a brief seizure with airway protection intact. Discharging before adequate recovery or physically restraining the patient are both inappropriate approaches to routine postictal care.
  178. The correct answer is D (Start lactulose to reduce ammonia and treat hepatic encephalopathy): Lactulose reduces ammonia absorption and is first line treatment for hepatic encephalopathy, especially when a precipitant like constipation is identified and also addressed. A high protein diet would worsen ammonia levels, and withholding treatment ignores a clear reversible cause of her confusion. Attributing symptoms only to withdrawal without addressing the markedly elevated ammonia risks leaving a treatable condition unmanaged.
  179. The correct answer is A (Obtain blood cultures and start empiric antibiotics without delay for suspected meningitis): Suspected bacterial meningitis requires empiric antibiotics started immediately after blood cultures are drawn, without waiting for lumbar puncture or imaging results, since delayed treatment significantly worsens outcomes. Waiting for LP results or imaging before treating risks a preventable poor outcome. A tension headache does not explain fever, neck stiffness, and photophobia together, and this combination should never be dismissed without workup.
  180. The correct answer is B (Start high flow 100 percent oxygen and consider hyperbaric oxygen if severe): Carbon monoxide poisoning is treated with high flow 100 percent oxygen to accelerate carboxyhemoglobin elimination, with hyperbaric oxygen considered for severe cases or specific risk factors. Standard pulse oximetry cannot distinguish carboxyhemoglobin from oxyhemoglobin, so a normal reading is falsely reassuring and should not guide treatment. Attributing symptoms to anxiety or discharging based on subjective improvement ignores the need for objective carboxyhemoglobin trending before safe discharge.
  181. The correct answer is C (Attribute the syncope to orthostatic hypotension and review his medications): Positive orthostatic vital signs with a benign trigger and normal ECG support orthostatic hypotension, often related to medications like antihypertensives, as the cause, warranting medication review. Cardiac catheterization and CT angiogram are not indicated without evidence pointing to those specific diagnoses. Assuming seizure or psychiatric causes without supporting history or exam findings would miss the more likely and treatable orthostatic explanation.
  182. The correct answer is D (Recognize this pattern as concerning for a high risk cardiac cause): syncope during exertion is a red flag for a structural or electrical cardiac cause even when the resting ECG obtained afterward looks unremarkable, since exercise-induced arrhythmias may not be captured on a single resting strip, and this warrants further cardiac workup rather than reassurance. Vasovagal syncope classically occurs with prolonged standing or emotional triggers, not exertion, making that diagnosis less likely here. Discharging without cardiology follow up in exertional syncope risks missing a potentially life threatening arrhythmia or structural lesion.
  183. The correct answer is A (Diagnose vasovagal syncope and provide reassurance with counseling on triggers): Classic prodromal symptoms with a situational trigger, quick recovery, and normal exam and ECG are typical of benign vasovagal syncope, appropriately managed with reassurance and trigger avoidance counseling. Electrophysiology study, head CT, and empiric antiarrhythmics are unnecessary invasive or inappropriate steps for this low risk, classic presentation. Reserving further workup for atypical or high risk features avoids unnecessary testing.
  184. The correct answer is B (A prolonged QT interval identified on his ECG): A prolonged QT interval combined with a family history of sudden death raises concern for an inherited arrhythmia syndrome and warrants urgent cardiology referral before discharge. A normal ECG, a single remote episode, a normal exam, or symptom resolution alone are reassuring rather than concerning findings. Identifying specific high risk ECG abnormalities is key to appropriately triaging syncope patients for urgent versus routine follow up.
  185. The correct answer is C (Admit for cardiac monitoring and evaluate for pacemaker placement): Mobitz type II second degree AV block causing syncope is a high risk finding requiring admission for monitoring and evaluation for pacemaker placement, since it can progress unpredictably to complete heart block and carries risk of recurrent syncope or sudden cardiac death. Outpatient follow up or attributing this to a benign vasovagal cause would miss a dangerous conduction abnormality. A beta blocker would further slow the heart rate and could worsen the block, making it clearly inappropriate here.
  186. The correct answer is D (Discharge home with reassurance and no further cardiac testing needed): Low risk syncope with a clear benign trigger, normal ECG, and normal exam can be safely discharged without further cardiac testing, based on validated risk stratification tools. Admitting, obtaining urgent echocardiography, or placing an implantable monitor are all excessive for a clearly low risk presentation. Reserving further workup for higher risk features avoids unnecessary cost and patient burden.
  187. The correct answer is D (Abnormal ECG, history of heart failure, and syncope during exertion): The combination of an abnormal ECG, known structural heart disease like heart failure, and exertional onset are all recognized high risk features warranting admission and further cardiac workup. A benign prodrome, normal ECG without cardiac history, prolonged standing in heat, or full symptom resolution with a reassuring exam are all low risk features supporting outpatient management instead. Combining multiple risk factors, not any single feature alone, drives the admission decision.
  188. The correct answer is A (Identify this rhythm as a likely cause requiring urgent intervention): A significant rhythm abnormality captured during symptoms strongly supports it as the cause of syncope and requires urgent cardiology involvement and likely intervention. Calling a clearly abnormal rhythm normal or attributing symptoms to anxiety ignores objective evidence of a cardiac cause. Discharging without addressing a documented dangerous rhythm risks a life threatening recurrence.
  189. The correct answer is B (Consider carotid sinus hypersensitivity as a potential cause): Syncope triggered by head turning or neck pressure suggests carotid sinus hypersensitivity, which can be further evaluated with carotid sinus massage under monitored conditions. This is a distinct entity from typical vasovagal syncope given the specific mechanical trigger. Dehydration or seizure do not explain this classic positional trigger pattern.
  190. The correct answer is C (Consider supine hypotensive syndrome from vena cava compression): In late pregnancy, supine positioning can cause the gravid uterus to compress the inferior vena cava, reducing venous return and causing syncope, which improves with left lateral positioning. Jumping to arrhythmia, preeclampsia, or epilepsy without considering this common, pregnancy specific and easily explained mechanism overlooks the most likely cause given the classic positional history.
  191. The correct answer is D (Secure and clear the airway as the first priority in the primary survey): The primary survey follows the ABCDE sequence, and airway management with cervical spine precautions always takes priority over other interventions in an unresponsive trauma patient. Imaging, extremity splinting, secondary survey, and catheter placement all come after the airway, breathing, and circulation have been addressed. Delaying airway management in a patient with gurgling respirations risks rapid deterioration from airway obstruction or aspiration.
  192. The correct answer is A (Recognize findings consistent with a pneumothorax requiring intervention): Findings consistent with pneumothorax identified during the primary survey's breathing assessment should prompt timely intervention such as chest tube placement, especially if the patient is hemodynamically or respiratory compromised. Calling clearly abnormal findings normal or attributing them to chronic disease without correlating with the acute trauma misses an urgent injury. Waiting for a full trauma series before addressing a breathing threat identified in the primary survey contradicts the sequential ABCDE approach.
  193. The correct answer is B (Evaluate for occult internal hemorrhage in the chest, abdomen, pelvis, or long bones): Unexplained hypotension and tachycardia in trauma should prompt a systematic search for occult hemorrhage in the chest, abdomen, pelvis, and long bones, since significant blood loss can occur without external signs. Attributing shock to pain or anxiety alone in a trauma patient with these vital signs is a dangerous assumption. Waiting for further symptoms or assuming a preexisting cardiac cause without workup risks missing a treatable source of hemorrhagic shock.
  194. The correct answer is C (A Glasgow Coma Scale of 8 or less with asymmetric pupils): A Glasgow Coma Scale of 8 or less combined with pupillary asymmetry is a classic red flag for significant intracranial injury and mandates urgent neuroimaging and often neurosurgical involvement during the disability portion of the primary survey. A normal GCS, symmetric reactive pupils, resolved confusion, or a normal respiratory rate are all reassuring findings, not concerning ones. Recognizing this specific high risk combination guides urgent escalation of care.
  195. The correct answer is D (Hypothermia worsens coagulopathy and increases trauma related mortality): Hypothermia contributes to the lethal triad of coagulopathy, acidosis, and hypothermia in trauma, each worsening the others and increasing mortality, so active warming after exposure is a critical part of trauma care. Claiming hypothermia has no significant effect or that warming is only about comfort understates a serious physiologic risk. Exposure and subsequent warming are directly linked steps within the same primary survey sequence, not unrelated actions.
  196. The correct answer is A (Suspect intra-abdominal hemorrhage and prepare for possible emergent surgery): A rigid, distended abdomen with hypotension after significant trauma strongly suggests intra-abdominal hemorrhage requiring rapid evaluation, such as bedside ultrasound, and preparation for possible emergent surgical intervention. Being awake and talking does not exclude significant internal bleeding, especially early in hemorrhagic shock. Delaying care for worsening pain or attributing exam findings to anxiety risks a preventable death from ongoing internal hemorrhage.
  197. The correct answer is E (Suspect cardiac tamponade and prepare for pericardiocentesis or thoracotomy): Beck's triad of distended neck veins, muffled heart sounds, and hypotension is classic for cardiac tamponade, requiring urgent pericardiocentesis or thoracotomy depending on the clinical setting. Anxiety and diuretics do not explain or address this life threatening physiology. While tension pneumothorax can present with distended neck veins, the specific combination with muffled heart sounds points more toward tamponade, and either way emergent bedside evaluation should not be delayed for formal echocardiography.
  198. The correct answer is B (Prepare for a surgical airway such as cricothyroidotomy if intubation fails): Severe facial trauma anticipated to make laryngoscopy difficult should prompt preparation for a surgical airway as a backup plan if standard intubation attempts fail, following a difficult airway algorithm. Repeated attempts without backup risk losing the airway entirely. Abandoning airway management or relying on bag valve mask ventilation indefinitely are not definitive solutions for a patient who needs a secured airway.
  199. The correct answer is C (Apply a pelvic binder as part of early hemorrhage control): Early application of a pelvic binder helps reduce pelvic volume and control hemorrhage from an unstable fracture, and should be done promptly as part of primary survey circulation management. Repeated manipulation can worsen bleeding by disrupting early clot formation. Delaying stabilization for imaging or prioritizing less urgent extremity injuries over hemodynamically significant pelvic hemorrhage would be inappropriate sequencing.
  200. The correct answer is D (Perform a thorough secondary survey to identify additional injuries): Once the primary survey is complete and life threats are addressed, a thorough head to toe secondary survey should follow to identify additional injuries that may not have been immediately apparent. Discharging or stopping evaluation after only the primary survey risks missing significant injuries. Repeating only the airway assessment ignores the need for a comprehensive examination of the entire patient.
  201. The correct answer is B (Send directly to the emergency department for urgent evaluation): new exertional chest tightness with a cardiac pattern in a man with unclear risk stratification warrants same-day emergency evaluation to rule out unstable angina or evolving infarction.   202.   203. The correct answer is D (Refer for hospital admission with full septic workup and empiric antibiotics): any fever of 38°C or higher in an infant younger than 28 days is considered a medical emergency regardless of how well the infant appears, because clinical appearance is unreliable for detecting serious bacterial infection in neonates. Standard practice is hospital admission with blood, urine, and cerebrospinal fluid cultures plus empiric antibiotics pending results.   204. The correct answer is A (Send to the emergency department for further management): a peak flow that remains at 55% of personal best after appropriate nebulizer treatments indicates a moderate to severe exacerbation that has not adequately responded to office-based therapy. This incomplete response signals need for closer monitoring, possible additional bronchodilator dosing, and evaluation for admission that exceeds outpatient clinic capability. Simple discharge or continued in-office observation risks deterioration, while ICU admission is premature without further reassessment.
  205. The correct answer is B (Give tetanus toxoid-containing vaccine only, no immune globulin needed): tetanus immune globulin is reserved for patients whose vaccination history is unknown or who have completed fewer than three prior tetanus toxoid doses, not simply based on time since the last dose. Since this patient has received routine boosters in the past, implying a completed primary series, a dirty wound more than five years since his last dose only requires an updated toxoid-containing vaccine booster today. Giving immune globulin as well is unnecessary in a fully immunized patient. Withholding all prophylaxis would ignore both the wound risk category and the outdated booster interval.
  206. The correct answer is C (Intramuscular epinephrine given immediately in the anterolateral thigh): anaphylaxis with hypotension and stridor is life-threatening and epinephrine is the first-line, most time-critical treatment that must be given without delay. Antihistamines, corticosteroids, and nebulized bronchodilators are useful adjuncts but do not reverse the vasodilation and airway edema as rapidly or effectively as epinephrine. Delaying epinephrine to give other agents first significantly increases the risk of cardiovascular collapse and death.
  207. The correct answer is C (Send emergently for evaluation and treatment of suspected hyperkalemia): peaked T waves with QRS widening in a dialysis patient who missed sessions are classic ECG findings of life-threatening hyperkalemia, which can rapidly progress to fatal arrhythmia. This requires emergent transfer for calcium stabilization, insulin-glucose therapy, and urgent dialysis, not outpatient labs or routine scheduling.   208. The correct answer is D (Moderate dehydration, begin oral rehydration therapy and reassess closely): sunken fontanelle, dry mucous membranes, decreased tears, and mildly delayed capillary refill with preserved alertness and eager drinking indicate moderate dehydration, which is typically manageable with closely monitored oral rehydration therapy rather than immediate IV fluids. Classifying this as mild would underestimate the exam findings, while severe dehydration typically includes lethargy, minimal urine output, and significantly delayed capillary refill. Close reassessment is essential to confirm response to oral rehydration.
  209. The correct answer is C (Send emergently for evaluation of central retinal artery occlusion): sudden painless monocular vision loss with a pale retina and afferent pupillary defect is the classic presentation of central retinal artery occlusion, a true ophthalmic and vascular emergency requiring immediate evaluation since retinal tissue infarcts within a short window. This is treated like an acute stroke of the eye and needs urgent stroke workup as well. Routine follow-up, topical steroids, or acetazolamide alone are inappropriate and would allow irreversible vision loss.
  210. The correct answer is D (Start empiric topical fluoroquinolone and refer urgently to ophthalmology): a corneal infiltrate with an overlying epithelial defect in a contact lens wearer is concerning for microbial keratitis, a sight-threatening condition requiring prompt broad-spectrum topical antibiotic coverage and urgent ophthalmology evaluation. Topical corticosteroids are contraindicated before infection is ruled out because they can worsen an infectious keratitis. Patching traps organisms against the cornea and is avoided in contact lens related infections, and reassurance with tears alone risks corneal perforation.
  211. The correct answer is D (Immediately refer for emergent urology evaluation for possible torsion): sudden severe testicular pain with a high-riding, horizontal testicle and absent cremasteric reflex are classic findings for testicular torsion, which is a surgical emergency requiring detorsion within hours to salvage the testicle. Waiting for ultrasound results delays definitive surgical care when clinical suspicion is already high.   212. The correct answer is A (Attempt gentle removal with direct visualization and appropriate instruments): a cooperative child with a visible, non-organic foreign body such as a bead in the anterior nasal cavity is appropriate for careful attempted removal in the primary care or urgent care setting using proper lighting and instruments such as a bayonet forceps or balloon catheter technique. Referral is reserved for failed attempts, posterior or button battery foreign bodies, or an uncooperative child.
