  213. The correct answer is C (Activate emergency medical services for immediate stroke center transfer): acute focal neurological deficits with a clear time of onset within the thrombolysis window represent a time-critical stroke emergency requiring immediate emergency transport to a facility capable of advanced imaging and potential clot-dissolving therapy. Every minute of delay for in-clinic exams, aspirin dosing, or observation reduces the chance of a good neurological outcome. Outpatient imaging is far too slow for a condition where time equals brain tissue.
  214. The correct answer is D (Cool the burn, cleanse, apply appropriate dressing, and manage pain outpatient): a small superficial partial-thickness burn without airway involvement, on a non-critical body area, and with stable vitals can typically be managed in the outpatient setting with cooling, cleansing, an appropriate non-adherent dressing, and analgesia. Burn center referral is reserved for larger burns, burns involving the face, hands, joints, or genitalia, or full-thickness injury. Direct prolonged ice application can worsen tissue injury, and prophylactic antibiotics are not routinely indicated for an uninfected burn.
  215. The correct answer is C (Leave the wound open, start prophylactic antibiotics, and assess rabies risk): deep puncture wounds from animal bites, especially on the hand, carry high infection risk and are generally left open or loosely approximated rather than tightly sutured, along with prophylactic antibiotic coverage for typical oral flora pathogens. The rabies exposure status of the animal must also be assessed to determine if post-exposure prophylaxis is needed. Primary closure of a high-risk bite wound increases the risk of abscess formation, and withholding antibiotics ignores the documented contamination risk.
  216. The correct answer is D (Send emergently for imaging and treatment of suspected orbital cellulitis): proptosis, painful restricted eye movements, and fever distinguish orbital cellulitis, a sight- and life-threatening infection extending behind the orbital septum, from simple periorbital cellulitis or conjunctivitis. This requires emergent imaging such as CT to assess for abscess and admission for intravenous antibiotics. Oral antibiotics alone or routine follow-up would not adequately treat this deep infection, which can progress to cavernous sinus thrombosis or vision loss.
  217. The correct answer is D (Acute angle-closure glaucoma, refer emergently for pressure-lowering treatment): severe eye pain, halos around lights, nausea, a fixed mid-dilated pupil, and a firm globe on palpation are classic for acute angle-closure glaucoma, an ophthalmic emergency that can cause permanent vision loss within hours if intraocular pressure is not urgently lowered. Neither conjunctivitis, migraine, nor a corneal abrasion produces this constellation of pupil and pressure findings. Emergent referral for pressure-lowering medications and possible laser iridotomy is required.
  218. The correct answer is B (This likely represents normal athletic bradycardia, no urgent workup needed): a resting heart rate in the 40s with an otherwise normal ECG in an asymptomatic, well-conditioned athlete without concerning family history is a common physiologic finding from high vagal tone and does not require emergent referral or pacemaker workup. Immediate emergency referral or starting a rate-slowing medication would be inappropriate and could cause harm in this benign, expected physiologic variant. Reassurance and routine follow-up are appropriate.
  219. The correct answer is D (Give nebulized racemic epinephrine and oral or IM dexamethasone, then observe): stridor at rest with retractions indicates moderate to severe croup, which should be treated promptly with nebulized racemic epinephrine for rapid symptom relief and corticosteroids to reduce airway inflammation, followed by a period of observation to ensure no rebound stridor. Discharge without treatment or antibiotics for a viral illness would not address the airway compromise. Attempting direct visualization with instruments in a child with stridor risks provoking complete airway obstruction and should be avoided.
  220. The correct answer is A (Call for emergency transfer for immediate synchronized cardioversion): a wide complex tachycardia with hypotension and altered mental status indicates unstable ventricular tachycardia, requiring emergent transfer for immediate synchronized cardioversion rather than any oral medication or observation. Vagal maneuvers and fluids alone will not correct this unstable rhythm, and outpatient imaging is far too slow for a hemodynamically unstable arrhythmia. Delaying definitive treatment risks cardiac arrest.
  221. The correct answer is B (Transfer emergently for further management of severe exacerbation): persistent hypoxia below 90%, inability to speak in full sentences, and accessory muscle use after initial treatment indicate a severe COPD exacerbation with impending respiratory failure that exceeds outpatient clinic capability. This requires emergency department evaluation for possible noninvasive ventilation, closer monitoring, and further therapy. Discharge with oral antibiotics alone or subjective improvement without objective reassessment risks respiratory arrest.
  222. The correct answer is A (Primary closure with sutures is appropriate given the low-risk clean wound): a clean laceration presenting within the golden period for closure, typically up to 6 to 8 hours on the extremities, with minimal contamination and no deep structure injury is appropriate for primary suture closure. Leaving it open or delaying closure unnecessarily increases healing time and scarring without added benefit for a low-risk wound. Topical ointment alone would not adequately approximate the wound edges for proper healing.
  223. The correct answer is A (Send emergently for evaluation of suspected severe preeclampsia): severe headache, visual disturbance, right upper quadrant pain, and severely elevated blood pressure in a pregnant patient are classic features of severe preeclampsia, which can rapidly progress to eclampsia, HELLP syndrome, or stroke and requires emergent obstetric evaluation and treatment. Outpatient labs, oral antihypertensives alone, or reassurance dangerously delay care for this obstetric emergency. Immediate transfer for maternal and fetal monitoring is required.
  224. The correct answer is A (Send emergently for evaluation and treatment of suspected diabetic ketoacidosis): markedly elevated glucose, large ketones, altered mental status, and hypotension together indicate diabetic ketoacidosis, a life-threatening emergency requiring hospital-based intravenous fluid resuscitation, insulin therapy, and electrolyte monitoring that cannot be safely managed with oral agents in clinic. Oral insulin dosing or outpatient A1c testing would not address the acute metabolic derangement. Immediate emergency transfer is necessary to prevent further deterioration.
  225. The correct answer is C (Send emergently for evaluation of suspected appendicitis without any delay): migratory periumbilical to right lower quadrant pain with fever, anorexia, and focal peritoneal signs at McBurney point is the classic presentation of acute appendicitis, requiring urgent surgical evaluation and imaging to prevent perforation. Viral gastroenteritis typically causes diffuse pain without focal peritoneal findings. Oral antibiotics alone or dietary modification would delay time-sensitive surgical management.
  226. The correct answer is A (Intussusception, send emergently for imaging and reduction as clinically indicated at this visit): episodic severe abdominal pain with leg drawing, intervening lethargy, and red currant jelly stool are the classic triad for intussusception, a surgical emergency requiring urgent imaging, typically ultrasound, and often air or contrast enema reduction. Gastroenteritis, constipation, milk protein allergy, and colic do not produce this specific pattern of bowel telescoping and bloody mucoid stool. Delayed diagnosis risks bowel ischemia and perforation.
  227. The correct answer is A (Pyloric stenosis, refer emergently for imaging and surgical evaluation): projectile non-bilious vomiting after feeds with continued hunger, in the classic age range of 3 to 6 weeks, along with a palpable olive-shaped mass, strongly suggests hypertrophic pyloric stenosis, which requires urgent ultrasound confirmation and surgical pyloromyotomy. Gastroesophageal reflux and gastroenteritis do not typically present with a palpable abdominal mass or projectile vomiting pattern. Delay risks worsening dehydration and metabolic alkalosis.
  228. The correct answer is C (Send emergently for evaluation of suspected ovarian torsion): sudden severe unilateral pelvic pain with nausea and vomiting in the setting of a known large ovarian cyst is highly concerning for ovarian torsion, a time-sensitive surgical emergency where delay risks loss of the ovary from ischemia. This requires emergent transfer for urgent ultrasound with Doppler and possible surgical detorsion, not a scheduled outpatient study. Oral medications or reassurance would dangerously delay definitive care.
  229. The correct answer is D (Aortic dissection, send emergently for imaging and surgical evaluation): tearing chest pain radiating to the back combined with a significant blood pressure discrepancy between arms is a classic and highly concerning presentation for aortic dissection, a surgical emergency requiring immediate imaging such as CT angiography and blood pressure control. None of the benign diagnoses listed would explain the interarm blood pressure differential. Any delay in recognizing this presentation can be fatal from aortic rupture.
  230. The correct answer is B (Apply topical vasoconstrictor, attempt nasal packing, and check coagulation status): persistent anterior epistaxis unresponsive to prolonged direct pressure, especially in an anticoagulated patient, warrants topical vasoconstrictor application and anterior nasal packing along with urgent assessment of coagulation status such as INR. Continuing pressure alone without further intervention risks ongoing significant blood loss. Simply stopping warfarin without addressing active bleeding or checking labs ignores both the acute hemorrhage and the thromboembolic risk of abrupt discontinuation.
  231. The correct answer is D (Prescribe topical antibiotic ointment and arrange follow-up in 24 to 48 hours): an isolated corneal abrasion confirmed with fluorescein staining and no evidence of penetrating injury or retained foreign body is typically managed conservatively with a topical antibiotic to prevent infection and close follow-up to confirm healing. Surgical repair is not indicated for a simple abrasion. Prolonged tight patching is no longer recommended as it can impair healing and increase infection risk, and topical corticosteroids are contraindicated as they can worsen epithelial healing and mask infection.
  232. The correct answer is B (Start oral antibiotics for cellulitis and mark the borders for monitoring): spreading erythema, warmth, and tenderness around a wound with associated low-grade fever are consistent with early cellulitis, which is appropriately managed with oral antibiotics targeting typical skin flora and marking the erythema borders to track response or progression. Reassurance without treatment risks progression to a deeper infection. Immediate surgical debridement is reserved for necrotizing infection or abscess, which are not described here, and topical treatment alone is inadequate for spreading cellulitis.
  233. The correct answer is A (Send emergently for evaluation of suspected bacterial meningitis): fever, severe headache, neck stiffness, photophobia, and a petechial rash together are highly concerning for bacterial meningitis, particularly meningococcemia, which can progress rapidly to septic shock and death without emergent treatment. This requires immediate emergency transfer for lumbar puncture, blood cultures, and prompt empiric antibiotics, not outpatient testing or oral medication. Any delay in a patient with this rash pattern significantly increases mortality risk.
  234. The correct answer is C (Activate emergency medical services for immediate transfer to a facility capable of reperfusion): ST elevations on ECG with classic ischemic symptoms indicate an ST-elevation myocardial infarction, a time-critical emergency where every minute of delay increases myocardial damage, requiring immediate transfer for emergent reperfusion therapy such as percutaneous coronary intervention. Waiting for troponin results, observing in clinic, or scheduling an outpatient visit would cause dangerous and potentially fatal delay in definitive treatment.
  235. The correct answer is B (Refer promptly for cardiology evaluation given symptomatic conduction disease): a progressively lengthening PR interval followed by a dropped beat describes Mobitz type I second degree heart block, and when associated with symptoms like near-syncope, it warrants prompt cardiology evaluation to assess for progression and possible pacemaker need. This is not a benign finding to dismiss when symptomatic. Starting a calcium channel blocker would further slow conduction and could worsen symptoms, and increasing caffeine is not an appropriate management strategy for conduction disease.
  236. The correct answer is C (Send urgently for same-day evaluation given high stroke risk after suspected TIA): recurrent transient focal neurological symptoms that fully resolve are classic for transient ischemic attacks, which carry a significant short-term risk of a completed stroke, especially with recurrent episodes, and require urgent same-day evaluation for imaging and secondary prevention. Reassurance or delaying workup to a monthly follow-up ignores this window of elevated risk. This presentation needs urgent risk stratification, not routine management alone.
  237. The correct answer is B (Give 15 to 20 grams of fast-acting oral glucose and recheck in 15 minutes): a conscious patient able to safely swallow with symptomatic hypoglycemia should first receive oral fast-acting glucose with a recheck in 15 minutes, following the standard rule of 15 approach. Intramuscular glucagon is reserved for patients who cannot safely take oral glucose, such as those who are unconscious or unable to swallow. Sending to the emergency department before attempting simple in-clinic treatment delays correction of a reversible condition, and giving insulin would dangerously worsen the hypoglycemia.
  238. The correct answer is A (Send emergently for evaluation of suspected pulmonary embolism): acute pleuritic chest pain and dyspnea after prolonged immobility combined with unilateral leg swelling strongly suggests deep vein thrombosis with possible pulmonary embolism, a potentially fatal condition requiring emergent imaging and anticoagulation. Reassurance, oral pain medication alone, or a delayed outpatient study would risk a fatal embolic event before diagnosis and treatment can occur. This presentation requires immediate emergency evaluation.
  239. The correct answer is B (Obtain an ankle X-ray per the Ottawa ankle rules given these findings): the Ottawa ankle rules recommend imaging when there is bone tenderness at the posterior edge of the malleolus or inability to bear weight for four steps both immediately after injury and in the clinic, both of which are present here. Simply reassuring without imaging risks missing a fracture. Casting without confirming a fracture on imaging is premature, and there is no indication of an open wound to warrant antibiotics or immediate surgical referral before basic imaging.
  240. The correct answer is A (Send emergently for evaluation and management of withdrawal seizure): a witnessed withdrawal seizure in a patient with alcohol use disorder requires emergent evaluation given the risk of recurrent seizures, progression to delirium tremens, and the need for monitored benzodiazepine dosing and possible inpatient management. Discharging home or managing this with oral medication alone in the outpatient setting is unsafe given the unpredictable trajectory of alcohol withdrawal. Recommending increased alcohol intake is medically inappropriate and unsafe.
  241. The correct answer is B (Send emergently for evaluation and controlled blood pressure reduction given end-organ symptoms): severely elevated blood pressure accompanied by symptoms such as severe headache and visual changes indicates hypertensive emergency with potential end-organ damage, requiring emergent transfer for careful, monitored blood pressure reduction rather than rapid oral dosing in clinic, which can cause dangerous overcorrection. This is not asymptomatic hypertension, which would be managed differently as an urgency. Simply resting or outpatient follow-up ignores the symptomatic end-organ involvement.
  242. The correct answer is D (Send emergently for evaluation of suspected retinal detachment): a curtain-like visual field loss along with the described folds and elevation of retinal tissue on fundoscopy is classic for retinal detachment, a time-sensitive emergency requiring urgent ophthalmologic evaluation and surgical repair to preserve vision, particularly before the detachment progresses to involve the macula. Routine follow-up, topical steroids, or simple rest would allow progression to permanent vision loss. Diabetic patients are also at elevated risk, further supporting urgent referral.
  243. The correct answer is A (Refer for orthopedic evaluation and imaging given suspected ligament injury): immediate swelling, a popping sensation, inability to bear weight, and a positive Lachman test strongly suggest an anterior cruciate ligament tear, which requires orthopedic referral and MRI for confirmation and treatment planning. Reassuring as a minor sprain would miss a significant ligamentous injury. Casting without further workup or immediate return to sport risks further joint damage, and there is no indication described that suggests infection to warrant antibiotics.
  244. The correct answer is C (Send emergently for immediate imaging and removal given battery ingestion): button battery ingestion is a true emergency regardless of current symptoms because the battery can cause severe esophageal burns and perforation within hours through electrical current generation, requiring emergent imaging to localize the battery and urgent endoscopic removal if lodged in the esophagus. Waiting for symptoms to develop or scheduling delayed imaging risks catastrophic tissue injury. Inducing vomiting and activated charcoal are not appropriate and do not address the risk of ongoing tissue damage.
  245. The correct answer is B (Perform incision and drainage of the abscess with appropriate wound care): a fluctuant, well-localized abscess without systemic toxicity is appropriately managed in the outpatient or urgent care setting with incision and drainage, which is the definitive treatment, sometimes with adjunctive antibiotics for surrounding cellulitis or risk factors. Antibiotics alone without drainage are typically insufficient for a fluctuant abscess. Emergency department referral is unnecessary for a straightforward abscess that can be managed in clinic, and warm compresses alone would delay definitive source control.
  246. The correct answer is D (Send emergently for evaluation of suspected ectopic pregnancy): unilateral abdominal pain, vaginal bleeding, a positive pregnancy test, and orthostatic symptoms in early pregnancy are highly concerning for ectopic pregnancy with possible rupture, a life-threatening emergency requiring immediate evaluation with ultrasound and possible surgical intervention. Reassurance, iron supplementation, or routine scheduled imaging would dangerously delay diagnosis of a potentially catastrophic intra-abdominal hemorrhage. This presentation demands emergent transfer.
  247. The correct answer is C (Discharge home with pain control, hydration advice, and imaging as an outpatient): classic renal colic symptoms with hematuria in an afebrile patient whose pain is well controlled can typically be managed as an outpatient with analgesia, hydration, straining of urine, and outpatient imaging such as a CT scan to confirm stone size and location. Emergency referral or admission is reserved for uncontrolled pain, fever suggesting obstruction with infection, or solitary kidney concerns. This presentation does not describe a urinary tract infection, so antibiotics alone would be inappropriate.
  248. The correct answer is C (Reassure regarding likely vasovagal syncope and provide precautionary counseling): a classic prodrome of warmth, nausea, and tunnel vision triggered by a known vasovagal stimulus such as blood donation, followed by rapid recovery and normal vital signs and ECG, is consistent with benign vasovagal syncope, not a cardiac or neurological emergency. Admission, emergent cardiology referral, or head imaging are not warranted for this classic benign presentation with reassuring findings. A beta blocker is not indicated for typical vasovagal syncope.
  249. The correct answer is B (Send emergently for evaluation of suspected subarachnoid hemorrhage): a thunderclap headache reaching maximal intensity within seconds, especially the worst headache of a patient's life, combined with meningismus, is a classic presentation for subarachnoid hemorrhage from aneurysm rupture, a neurosurgical emergency requiring immediate imaging and further workup. Treating this as a benign tension or migraine headache with oral medications and outpatient follow-up risks missing a catastrophic and potentially fatal intracranial event.
  250. The correct answer is D (Send emergently for evaluation of suspected digoxin toxicity): new symptoms of fatigue and dizziness in a patient on digoxin, combined with new ectopy on ECG and a missed recent lab check, raise concern for digoxin toxicity, which can cause life-threatening arrhythmias and requires emergent evaluation including a digoxin level, electrolytes, and cardiac monitoring. The scooped ST segment alone is an expected digoxin effect, but new ectopy with symptoms suggests toxicity rather than a benign finding. Increasing the dose or delaying labs would be dangerous.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Send emergently for joint aspiration and evaluation of suspected septic arthritis): fever, significant joint swelling, warmth, and inability to bear weight following a recent skin infection are concerning for septic arthritis, a joint-threatening and potentially life-threatening emergency requiring urgent joint aspiration for diagnosis and prompt antibiotic therapy. Treating this as a simple sprain with rest, anti-inflammatory medication, or immobilization alone would allow ongoing joint destruction and risk of sepsis. Time to treatment directly affects long-term joint outcomes.
  2. The correct answer is D (Send emergently for evaluation and treatment of suspected upper gastrointestinal bleed): active hematemesis with hypotension and tachycardia in a patient with known cirrhosis raises high concern for a variceal or other significant upper gastrointestinal hemorrhage, a hemodynamically unstable emergency requiring immediate transfer for resuscitation and emergent endoscopy. Outpatient management, oral medications, or delayed endoscopy scheduling would be dangerously inadequate given the unstable vital signs and risk of rapid decompensation.
  3. The correct answer is D (Send emergently for imaging and evaluation of suspected recurrent pneumothorax): sudden pleuritic chest pain, dyspnea, decreased breath sounds, and hypoxia in a patient with a prior history of pneumothorax on the same side strongly suggests recurrence, which can progress to a tension pneumothorax and requires emergent imaging and possible chest tube placement. Reassurance as anxiety, oral medication alone, or delayed outpatient imaging would risk respiratory decompensation from an expanding pneumothorax.
  4. The correct answer is B (Manage locally with ice, elevation, and oral antihistamine, then observe): an isolated local reaction to an insect sting without any systemic or airway symptoms is not anaphylaxis and can be safely managed with local measures such as ice, elevation, and an oral antihistamine, followed by a period of observation for any delayed systemic symptoms. Epinephrine is reserved for signs of a systemic allergic reaction or anaphylaxis, which are absent here. Emergency referral or hospital admission is unnecessary for an uncomplicated local reaction.
  5. The correct answer is C (Open and explore the wound, obtain a wound culture, and start antibiotics): fever, purulent drainage, and worsening erythema around a surgical wound indicate a surgical site infection requiring wound exploration to assess for abscess or dehiscence, culture to guide antibiotic therapy, and initiation of empiric antibiotics. This is not expected postoperative healing, which would show progressive improvement rather than worsening signs. Simple dressing changes or increased activity would not address an active infectious process.
  6. The correct answer is E (Send emergently for evaluation of suspected mesenteric ischemia): severe abdominal pain out of proportion to exam findings in a patient with atrial fibrillation, a known embolic risk factor, along with bloody stool, is a classic and highly concerning presentation for acute mesenteric ischemia, a surgical emergency with high mortality if not rapidly diagnosed and treated. Gastroenteritis would not typically cause pain this severe relative to exam findings. Outpatient workup or symptomatic treatment alone would allow progression to bowel infarction.
  7. The correct answer is A (Epididymitis, start appropriate oral antibiotics and arrange follow-up): a gradual onset of scrotal pain and swelling over days with fever, pain relief with scrotal elevation, and an intact cremasteric reflex is more consistent with epididymitis than torsion, which typically presents with sudden severe pain and an absent cremasteric reflex. Appropriate oral antibiotic therapy targeting likely pathogens along with follow-up is the correct management. A testicular tumor would not typically present acutely with fever, and an inguinal hernia would present differently on exam.
  8. The correct answer is A (Obtain facial imaging and refer for evaluation of suspected orbital fracture): diplopia with restricted upward gaze, periorbital ecchymosis, and infraorbital numbness following facial trauma are concerning for an orbital floor fracture with possible extraocular muscle entrapment, which requires prompt facial CT imaging and surgical evaluation to prevent permanent diplopia or vision compromise. Simple reassurance, ice alone, or antibiotics without imaging would miss this significant structural injury requiring timely intervention.
  9. The correct answer is A (Send emergently for evaluation and treatment of suspected sepsis): new confusion, fever, hypotension, and tachycardia following a recent urinary catheter placement are concerning signs of sepsis, likely from a catheter-associated urinary tract infection, requiring emergent transfer for fluid resuscitation, blood cultures, and prompt broad-spectrum antibiotics. Oral antibiotics alone, outpatient culture results, or reassurance would dangerously delay treatment of a potentially rapidly progressing, life-threatening infection with hemodynamic instability already present.
  10. The correct answer is E (Refer emergently for hand surgery evaluation given high-pressure injection injury): high-pressure injection injuries can cause extensive deep tissue damage, chemical injury, and compartment syndrome despite a deceptively small entry wound, and require emergent hand surgery evaluation for surgical debridement regardless of how benign the wound appears externally. Treating this as a minor puncture with a simple bandage, antibiotics alone, or splinting significantly underestimates the risk of severe tissue necrosis and potential digit or hand loss.
  11. The correct answer is A (Bell palsy, start oral corticosteroids promptly and arrange follow-up): isolated facial weakness affecting the entire side of the face including forehead involvement and inability to close the eye, without limb weakness or sensory changes, is classic for Bell palsy, which is treated with early corticosteroids to improve recovery outcomes. A central stroke typically spares the forehead due to bilateral cortical innervation and often involves limb findings, which are absent here. Trigeminal neuralgia causes sharp facial pain rather than weakness, and this presentation does not fit multiple sclerosis exacerbation.
  12. The correct answer is B (Treat presumptively for an acute gout flare with appropriate anti-inflammatory therapy): a classic presentation of exquisite pain, redness, and swelling of the first metatarsophalangeal joint with a history of similar prior episodes strongly suggests recurrent gout, which can typically be managed in the outpatient setting with appropriate anti-inflammatory therapy without requiring emergency joint aspiration, provided there are no signs of systemic infection or atypical features. Empiric antibiotics without evaluation would be inappropriate absent signs of infection, and bed rest alone would not adequately address the inflammatory pain.
  13. The correct answer is A (Refer immediately for emergent surgical detorsion given confirmed torsion): absent blood flow on Doppler ultrasound in the setting of acute severe testicular pain confirms testicular torsion, and this is a true surgical emergency requiring immediate operative detorsion, as testicular salvage rates decline rapidly with each additional hour of ischemia. Absent flow is never a normal variant, and antibiotics, ice, or delayed follow-up would allow irreversible testicular infarction. Time to surgery is the single most important factor in outcome.
  14. The correct answer is A (Send emergently for evaluation and treatment of suspected decompensated heart failure): progressive dyspnea, orthopnea, hypoxia, crackles, and jugular venous distension together indicate acute decompensated heart failure with volume overload, requiring emergent evaluation for intravenous diuresis, oxygen support, and cardiac workup that exceeds outpatient clinic capability. Outpatient oral diuretics, dietary counseling alone, or compression stockings would not adequately address this level of respiratory compromise and fluid overload.
  15. The correct answer is C (Send emergently for evaluation and treatment of suspected ventricular tachycardia): a wide complex tachycardia with chest pain following stimulant use is a life-threatening arrhythmia requiring emergent transfer for cardiac monitoring and possible cardioversion or antiarrhythmic therapy, as it can rapidly deteriorate into cardiac arrest. Beta blockers are generally avoided in cocaine-associated cardiac emergencies due to risk of unopposed alpha stimulation worsening coronary vasospasm. Observation alone or simple discharge would be dangerously inadequate for this unstable rhythm.
  16. The correct answer is D (Begin rapid active cooling measures immediately while arranging emergent transfer): heat stroke with altered mental status, hot dry skin, and markedly elevated core temperature is a life-threatening emergency where immediate aggressive cooling, such as ice water immersion or evaporative cooling, is the single most important intervention to reduce mortality, and this should begin immediately while arranging emergent transfer rather than waiting. Oral fluids alone are inadequate and unsafe given altered mentation, antipyretics are ineffective for heat stroke since the mechanism is not a hypothalamic fever, and warming blankets would worsen the hyperthermia.
  17. The correct answer is D (Send emergently for evaluation of suspected obstructing stone with infection): fever, rigors, hypotension, and flank pain together suggest an obstructing kidney stone complicated by infection, essentially an infected, obstructed urinary system, which is a urologic emergency requiring emergent imaging and often urgent decompression along with intravenous antibiotics. This differs from simple uncomplicated renal colic, which does not include signs of systemic infection and hemodynamic compromise. Outpatient management would risk progression to urosepsis.
  18. The correct answer is D (Refer to an ENT specialist for removal given the swelling and location): a foreign body deep in the canal against the tympanic membrane with surrounding swelling and signs of infection is higher risk for complications such as tympanic membrane injury during removal attempts, and is best managed by an ENT specialist with proper equipment and visualization. Attempting removal in a swollen, painful canal risks pushing the object further or causing injury, irrigation is contraindicated with organic or swelling-prone situations and unclear battery status, and objects in the ear canal do not reliably pass on their own.
  19. The correct answer is B (Send emergently for evaluation of suspected ruptured ectopic pregnancy): shoulder tip pain from diaphragmatic irritation by free intraperitoneal blood, along with hypotension and near-syncope in early pregnancy, is a classic and alarming presentation of a ruptured ectopic pregnancy with significant hemoperitoneum, a life-threatening surgical emergency. This requires immediate emergent transfer for resuscitation and surgery, not outpatient testing, oral medication, or delayed imaging, given the evidence of hemodynamic instability already present.
  20. The correct answer is B (Avoid agitating the child, keep him upright, and arrange emergent airway management): high fever, drooling, muffled voice, and a tripod positioning strongly suggest epiglottitis, a life-threatening airway emergency where any agitation or attempted throat examination can precipitate complete airway obstruction. The correct approach is to keep the child calm and in a position of comfort while arranging immediate transfer for controlled airway management in a monitored setting. Direct visualization with instruments, outpatient antibiotics, or in-clinic strep testing would be dangerous and could trigger fatal airway obstruction.
  21. The correct answer is C (Send emergently for evaluation of suspected abdominal aortic aneurysm rupture): a pulsatile abdominal mass with sudden severe pain radiating to the back, diaphoresis, and borderline blood pressure is highly concerning for a ruptured or rapidly expanding abdominal aortic aneurysm, an immediately life-threatening surgical emergency requiring emergent imaging and vascular surgery evaluation. Outpatient imaging, oral pain medication, or dietary recommendations would allow catastrophic hemorrhage and death before diagnosis and treatment.
  22. The correct answer is E (Peritonsillar abscess, refer emergently for drainage and further management): muffled voice, uvular deviation, and worsening dysphagia are classic findings of a peritonsillar abscess, which can compromise the airway and requires emergent drainage along with antibiotics. Viral pharyngitis, strep pharyngitis, mononucleosis, and allergic reactions do not typically cause uvular deviation or this degree of asymmetric swelling, making abscess the most likely and most urgent diagnosis requiring prompt intervention rather than outpatient management alone.
  23. The correct answer is B (Send emergently for evaluation of suspected hemorrhagic stroke): a sudden severe headache with new focal neurological deficits and markedly elevated blood pressure is highly concerning for an intracranial hemorrhage, a time-critical emergency requiring immediate imaging and neurosurgical evaluation. This is not consistent with a simple migraine given the new focal deficits. Oral medications, outpatient imaging, or in-clinic observation would cause dangerous delay in a condition where rapid deterioration is common.
  24. The correct answer is C (Refer emergently to ophthalmology given signs of penetrating injury): a visible metallic fragment with an irregularly shaped pupil, low intraocular pressure, and a shallow anterior chamber are concerning signs of a penetrating globe injury, which is an ophthalmic emergency requiring emergent specialist evaluation and imaging, with any manipulation in clinic avoided as it can worsen the injury and cause extrusion of intraocular contents. Attempting removal, vigorous irrigation, or firm patching without urgent referral could severely worsen a globe rupture.
  25. The correct answer is C (Send emergently for imaging and evaluation of suspected cauda equina syndrome): saddle anesthesia, urinary retention, and bilateral leg weakness following an acute back injury are red flag findings for cauda equina syndrome, a surgical emergency requiring emergent MRI and prompt decompression to prevent permanent neurological damage including bowel and bladder dysfunction. Treating this as a simple strain with rest, muscle relaxants, or outpatient physical therapy would allow irreversible nerve damage to occur while awaiting delayed care.
  26. The correct answer is D (Send emergently for evaluation and treatment of suspected severe pneumonia with sepsis): new confusion, hypoxia, fever, and hypotension in the setting of a productive cough suggest severe community-acquired pneumonia complicated by sepsis, meeting criteria that indicate a high risk of mortality and the need for hospital-level care including intravenous antibiotics and fluid resuscitation. Oral antibiotics alone or outpatient imaging would be dangerously inadequate given the evidence of organ dysfunction and hemodynamic instability already present.
  27. The correct answer is A (Send emergently for evaluation given a first unprovoked seizure): a first-time unprovoked seizure in an adolescent, even with full return to baseline, warrants emergent evaluation to identify potential underlying causes such as structural lesions, metabolic derangements, or infection, along with appropriate imaging and further workup that is not feasible in the outpatient urgent care setting. Discharging without any further evaluation, starting medication without a diagnostic workup, or delaying to a routine outpatient visit would miss a potentially treatable underlying cause.
  28. The correct answer is B (Send emergently for airway evaluation given suspected drug-induced angioedema): progressive lip, tongue, and airway swelling after starting an ACE inhibitor, without urticaria, is classic for bradykinin-mediated angioedema, which does not respond reliably to antihistamines or epinephrine the way allergic reactions do and can rapidly progress to complete airway obstruction, requiring emergent airway management. Continuing the causative medication or simple oral antihistamine treatment without emergent evaluation would be dangerous given the risk of airway compromise.
  29. The correct answer is E (Send urgently for same-day evaluation given high near-term stroke risk after suspected TIA): a transient ischemic attack, even with complete resolution and a normal exam, carries a significant short-term risk of progressing to a completed stroke, particularly within the first 48 hours, and requires urgent same-day evaluation for brain and vascular imaging along with initiation of secondary prevention. Reassurance based on a normal current exam, routine follow-up, or delayed outpatient imaging ignores this critical early risk window.
  30. The correct answer is C (Send emergently for imaging and evaluation of suspected postoperative abscess): worsening pain, fever, and localized tenderness a week after appendectomy raise concern for a postoperative intra-abdominal abscess, which requires prompt imaging such as CT to confirm and often percutaneous or surgical drainage along with antibiotics. This pattern of worsening rather than improving symptoms is not expected normal postoperative healing. Oral pain medication alone or recommending increased activity would delay diagnosis of a significant postoperative complication.
  31. The correct answer is A (Refer promptly for evaluation of symptomatic bradycardia and possible pacing): a persistently slow heart rate in the high 30s associated with symptoms such as lightheadedness and fatigue indicates symptomatic bradycardia, which requires prompt cardiology evaluation to determine the underlying cause and assess the need for pacemaker placement. This rate is not a normal expected finding regardless of age when symptomatic. A beta blocker would further slow the heart rate and worsen symptoms, and recommending exercise would not address the underlying conduction abnormality.
  32. The correct answer is C (Begin immediate copious ocular irrigation before further examination): chemical eye exposure is a true ocular emergency where immediate copious irrigation with saline or available neutral fluid must begin before any other assessment, including visual acuity testing or detailed examination, because ongoing chemical contact causes progressive and potentially irreversible corneal and ocular surface damage with every passing minute. Delaying irrigation for visual acuity testing, anesthetic application, or arranging referral before treatment would allow continued tissue injury from the chemical agent.
  33. The correct answer is A (Periorbital cellulitis, start oral antibiotics and arrange close follow-up): eyelid swelling and redness without fever, pain with eye movement, or restricted extraocular movements suggests periorbital cellulitis, an infection anterior to the orbital septum, which can typically be managed with oral antibiotics and close outpatient follow-up rather than the emergent imaging and admission required for orbital cellulitis. The absence of painful eye movement and normal vision are key distinguishing features from the more dangerous orbital cellulitis, which involves structures behind the septum.
  34. The correct answer is B (Send emergently for evaluation of suspected necrotizing soft tissue infection): pain out of proportion to exam findings, rapidly spreading erythema, skin discoloration, and crepitus on palpation are red flag findings for necrotizing fasciitis, a rapidly progressive and life-threatening infection requiring emergent surgical evaluation for debridement along with broad-spectrum antibiotics. Oral antibiotics alone, topical treatment, or conservative measures would allow this rapidly fatal infection to progress, as surgical source control is the definitive treatment and delay significantly increases mortality.
  35. The correct answer is D (Refer urgently for further cardiac evaluation given possible anginal equivalent): exertional jaw pain and fatigue that improve with rest represent a possible anginal equivalent, especially in a 60-year-old, and a normal resting ECG does not rule out significant coronary artery disease, warranting urgent further cardiac evaluation such as stress testing. Attributing this to dental pain or simply providing analgesics and routine follow-up risks missing underlying unstable coronary disease that could progress to a myocardial infarction without appropriate workup.
  36. The correct answer is E (Send emergently for imaging given suspected intracranial abscess from sinus spread): new focal neurological deficits, fever, and headache in a patient with chronic sinusitis raise significant concern for intracranial spread of infection, such as a brain abscess or subdural empyema, a neurosurgical emergency requiring emergent imaging and prompt treatment. Treating this as simple sinusitis with decongestants or oral antibiotics alone, or delaying with outpatient imaging, would miss a life-threatening intracranial infectious process requiring urgent intervention.
  37. The correct answer is D (Send emergently for evaluation, acetaminophen level, and possible antidote treatment): acetaminophen overdose is frequently asymptomatic in the early hours despite ongoing hepatotoxic risk, and management depends on a timed acetaminophen level plotted against a treatment nomogram to determine need for antidote therapy, which must occur within a specific window to prevent liver failure. Reassurance based on feeling well, home monitoring, or symptomatic treatment alone would risk missing the critical treatment window for a potentially fatal but treatable overdose.
  38. The correct answer is B (Send emergently for ophthalmology evaluation given persistent elevated pressure and optic nerve findings): persistently elevated intraocular pressure with new optic nerve pallor and continued severe pain days after an untreated angle-closure episode indicates ongoing optic nerve damage from sustained elevated pressure, a true ophthalmic emergency requiring immediate pressure-lowering treatment and possible surgical intervention to prevent permanent blindness. Reassurance, oral pain medication, rest, or antibiotics would allow continued irreversible optic nerve damage to occur.
  39. The correct answer is A (Send emergently for urologic evaluation given obstruction with infection): an obstructing stone with confirmed hydronephrosis and fever represents an infected, obstructed urinary system, a true urologic emergency requiring urgent decompression, typically with a stent or nephrostomy tube, along with intravenous antibiotics, as this combination can rapidly progress to urosepsis. Outpatient management with oral antibiotics, fluids, or alpha blocker therapy alone would be inadequate and dangerous given the combination of obstruction and systemic infection already present.
  40. The correct answer is A (Cardiac tamponade, send emergently for evaluation and pericardiocentesis): Beck's triad of hypotension, distended neck veins, and muffled heart sounds following a recent myocardial infarction is classic for cardiac tamponade, potentially from a free wall rupture or hemorrhagic pericardial effusion, requiring emergent echocardiography and pericardiocentesis to relieve pressure on the heart. This is a distinctly different and far more urgent presentation than simple pericarditis, recurrent infarction, or anxiety, none of which explain this specific triad of findings.
  41. The correct answer is A (Send emergently for evaluation given the potential severity of iron toxicity): iron overdose in children can be life-threatening and classically has a deceptive early asymptomatic phase before progressing to severe gastrointestinal, cardiovascular, and metabolic complications, so any significant potential ingestion requires emergent evaluation with iron levels and imaging regardless of current well appearance. Activated charcoal does not effectively bind iron and is not indicated, and inducing vomiting at home is not recommended and could cause aspiration without providing reliable decontamination.
  42. The correct answer is E (Send emergently for ophthalmology evaluation given suspected retinal detachment): a sudden shower of floaters and flashes followed by a curtain-like visual field defect is a classic warning sign of retinal detachment, which requires emergent ophthalmologic evaluation since prompt surgical repair before the detachment reaches the macula significantly improves visual outcomes. While isolated floaters can sometimes be benign, this specific combination with a new visual field defect is a true emergency, and delaying with topical drops, rest, or outpatient testing risks permanent vision loss.
  43. Antihistamines, corticosteroids, and bronchodilators are useful adjuncts but do not reverse the underlying pathophysiology as quickly or effectively as epinephrine.
